LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE,

With the wane of the summer sun the days get shorter and colder, and something warmer is required than a month ago. Fashion generally is disporting herself in thicker apparel and the thin- ness of the summer dress is yielding to that of autumn. On the shoulders dresses are now abaci- lutely simple and severe. As yet some women cannot persuade themszlves to do without some little frill or trimming-. They fancy they look dowdy with an intcrrupted line from the neck to the elbow. I warn you against allowing your dressmaker persuading you into having shoulder-knots or frills. The whole trend of fashion just now is against them. And in eleeves as well, tightness and absence of trimming rule the hour. The only varieties are found in bands of lace inserted or a few tucks or rows of stitching on the top of the sleeves and at the cuff.

A sensible hat is an absolute iiecessity, for a smart hat will make the quietest, neatest costume chic. I feel inclined to resist the omnipresence of the sailor hat, for how deadly tired we are of it. Nevertheless, when it is worn, I own I prefer the plain black band to the college colours so often worn, but not owned. Fashionable women never wear the sailor hat in its naked form, though they are distinctly partial to it in a smarter and more feminine guise. One I saw the other day was simply a sailor trimmed with blue taffeta silk, striped with bobs ribbon velvet, a black quill thrust through the large chou, which was run, as all good choux are to-day, on one edge. Anothez hat was of browny-white rustic straw, with a scarf of maize-coloured tulle draped round it, into a large knot at one side, caught with a gold buckle through which a couple of black quills are thrust. The quill is just as fashionable to-day as it was yesterday, and generally finds a place in such simple hats as these.

I like always to eee some toech of white or cream colour about the throat—this gives a fresh and dainty air. The clear lace neck-bands that have been so muoh worn this year have a particular charm, and the lace scarves tied twice round the bare throat have shared it. Nothing we wear round our throats should be heavy, or it frames the face most unbecomingly ; hard lines of deep or bright colour should be avoided, for the more hazy and light the ending of the collar and the com- mencement of the neck the better. The tulle and mousseline scarvse fixed to the back of the hat are very becoming, but the black velvet strings are apt to be a little hard. I will try to describe a very chic little searf a little cousin of mine has been malting for herself, and which looks charming passed twice round the throat, the ends brought to tho front., and worn with a pink silk, or blue or white muslin ehirt. This cravat is of white mous- seLne de eche. The flower is made by an applica- tion of rose-coloured mousseline de soie, hemmed on with a creel stitch of white silk, and the centre embroidered in yellow. The leaves and stalk are outlined in green silk. The white muslin is cut away under the rose up to the two edges by which the flower is held to the muslin either side.

The serious downpour of rain he ':orought out quite a multitude of white wa,erproofs, some trimmed with a piping of red or blue. They look smart on cycles, but I do not care for them other- wise. They tire too short and "baggy." A really smart waterproof seems to be impossible, and for this reason I think many of on prefer to do without them. The new skirt made quite plain at the hack, which is guiltless of pleat or seam, looks very well it made quite short. A friend of mine had one made for travelling, and it wa quite a (multi% So, after all, there is come hope for our winter skirts. -

Sneaking of skirts reminds me of the fad that my readers must be eareful, ii they study eeonomy, to buy double width and reversible materials. The skirts made with a seam up the back or front are not vary "fetching," neither are they very new. Flannel shirts, or blouses—for the shirt proper is a thing of the past—are really pretty, parAeularly those Trade in cream, pale blue, or pale pink flannel. They have discarded the stiff linen collar and cnff to sport yekts of cross-barred narrow black velvet, and the collars and cuffs have trimmings of the same. A rumour of the coming popularity of green I do lint credit—a deep wine colour will be the faecurite shade, but most of us recognise the advantages of wearikg black in the autumn. There is always a certain vogue for green plaid, and for a rlrese :or a child the tartan of the 42nd clan should be ch J9en, with a little Wiijte chernisette at the neck easily taken out for washing and replaced ; this is one of the most durable and one of the most be- conAing styles. Tnere is a novelty promised in skirts. These are to be out.. in numerous gores in the old um- breLtt style, fitting quite tightly round the hips, spreading outwards at the hem, a style it is possible to shape with elegance in a comfortable walking length, therefore may we devoutly hope for its arrival. Your loving JENNIL

( Copyrig ht. ) THE

REVENGE OF VALERIE:

A ROMANCE OF BRITISH COLUMBIA.

BY HITME NISBET,

Author of "A Sweet Sinner," "Bail Up," "The Rebel Chief," "Kings of the Sea," "Hunting for Gold, "The Swampers," Jo.

CHAPTER XXI. 'VICTIMS 02' ?ASHTON.

This pretty winged Gadfly had already flown over Waters where Maxims and Gatlings and long-range guns were not allowed to lie idly under their water- proofs. In spite of our vaunted nineteenth century security on the waterways of the world, there are portions where the pirate and the cut-throat still flourish—demons much more remorseless and cruel than those that infested the Spanish Main. . . _ .

But the Duchess Valerie NCas not at all afraid. Her dainty pleasure ship could be turned into a veritable gun-boat on the shortest notice. Her gallant crew could rake the enemy fore and aft, while to any small shot or the defective old, fashioned grape guns of the Malay prows 1r Moorish galleys, the Gadfly was bullet-proof. Time was no great object with them for the pre- sent. They had fully three months before the Yukon River would be open, and the steam yacht was able to travel, with the anthracite and smoke- less fuel, twenty-three knots per hour easily when put upon her mettle. They were going for a pleasure trip, however, not a chase ; therefore, they nsed their ordinary coal, and called for fresh supplies at every available port, reserving their special coal for emergencies. Two weeks after the Gadfly left Cowes, Mr. Stephen Belwood despatched a serviceable ocean tramp round the same direction. The tramp was laden with plant, and carried miners engaged for three years' service, to work the new claims. They were to sail round America and wait for the Gadfly at the mouth of the Yukon River, in order to let the steam yacht go in first. The private arrange- ments of the Duchess and the company promoters made this necessary. Having, therefore, plenty of leisure on their hands, Commander Osborn steered straight towards Lisbon as his first coaling station. Down the Channel and through the Bay of Biscay—ah I that tempest-tossed Bay of Biscay. The Gadfly was beautifully adapted to pass over two waves at a time, but the waves of Biscay defy even the best- laid schemes of shipbuilders. Here the company did penance for their gastronomic sins, past and present. The anthracite coal was piled on the furnaces, and the Gadfly darted through the seething green waves at her swiftest ; yet there were many miserable souls on board during that two days' passage. Beds were more in demand, with basins, than baths and boudoirs. At this portion of the voyage his lord- ship the right reverend Bishop and the two eagle- nosed dowagers had grave doubts as to their physician's goodwill towards them. While they lay groaning in their cabins in their dire extremity they were inclined to entertain suspicions as to the integrity of this fashionable physician, who pocketed his guineas so freely. Supposing, after all, he was a secret emissary of that dreadful Radical, John Burns, and meant to assassinate the upper classes under the pretence that they required ozone?

They bad the ozone with a vengeance now, but ag it was administered, dynamite could not have been much worse.

"I have my suspicions of Sir Harold Goutsettler, " murmured his reverend lordship wearily, as he raised himself from the basin which the respectful steward had hAti his dignified head over. "He has been raised fr.iin the lower ranks, and doubtless retains sonic sympathy still with those dangerous principles of the masses from which he sprang." The good Bishop was as unjust to the fashionable physician in his dire agony as Hope Eastlake hyd been to the rector.

When, however, the Gadfly hove in sight of the Spanish coasts, the weather swiftly cleared and the waves became beautifully less. Then the Bishop and the stately dowagers began to take a more reasonable view of affairs mundane, and even be interested so far as to come up on deck and take a good quaff of the ozone recommended. They all felt better after this, and began to take an active interest in the unfolding scenery and the bill of fare for luncheon.

It had not been a long purgatory for the paradise which they were now enjoying. Three days since they had shivered before a marrow-piercing north- east wind, which not all their wrappings could shut out. Now they were gliding over dark-blue waters, where the small waves tossed snowy crests into the warm sunshine.

It was almost pleasure enough to lie back on those comfortable deck chairs and watch those wavelets through the netting; every moment brought an alteration and variety of lighting and colour. Emerald green, amethyst, violet and sapphire-like blue danced over the ocean as the sunlight shifted and raced. Away in the distance stole out cape after cape of the tawny coasts of Portugal, the softest and tenderest of mist-films towards the horizon, bluffing out bolder and more definite of colour as the yacht neared them; until what had been soft washes became rugged cliffs all red and yellow in the lights, and royal purple in the shadows.

Spring costumes took the place of winter furs, those to be swiftly succeeded by summer gauziness. To those gay butterflies of fashion the rapid changes were delightful. These gave them the occupation to which they were accustomed, and without which occupation life would be so void and dreary. They disappeared at regular intervals into their cabins as actors and actresses do behind the wings; to reappear exhibiting fresh marvels of the sartorial craft. Like birds of gayest plumage, they promenaded the deck, spreading abroad the supposed spices of Araby. They completely altered the original briny flavour of the ozone, so that it wafted over their cheeks and into the nostrils of the seamen charged with violet, cherry blossom, and a thousand delicate and subtle essences of flowers, making them dream of heaven. The good Bishop, accustomed wherever he went to those celestial odours, was obstinate in his opinion regarding the aroma of ozone. A slight— very slight-bouquet of salt might be discernable at times, through the more exquisite blendings ; bat he was positive that the ocean air was impregnated with the same qualities as that which wafted in early summer over gardens and lawns. It was the one serious and imperative business of these voyagers' lives afloat or on shore to dress themselves or, rather, have themselves dressed. Vanity had but little to do with it. Modesty nothing. Fashion had fixed laws which dominated every other consideration. The ladies dressed for the promenade between their bedrooms and the bath in charming deshabille. They dressed for breakfast in chaste and simple cut garbs and shades. As the hours advanced towards dinner-time, so they became more extravagant and butterfly-like ; starting with linen, muslins and softly-clinging materials, to close the exhibition with silks and satins and other rare stuffs.

In the morning they covered their necks and arms with jealous decorum. For luncheon they permitted the gentlemen and seamen to observe slightly-veiled glimpses of those charms, so as gradually to accustom them to the evening exhibition. At dinner, and afterwards, they flung shyness to the perfume-charged ocean breezes and appeared in all their bravery of innocence. The transformation scene was on then. The gentlemen could see for themselves then whether their friends had been good judges of the feminine form divine when they bestowed their names upon those sirens. The young men could examine the wares that were offered for their inspection ; while the honest tars, as they looked, then turned their eyes away bashful, thought about the wives, sl,veethearts and sisters they had left at home. It was a fair and dazzling picture when the electric globes were glowing. fgenerous and lavish display of rich and artful robes, with flashing jewellery, resting upon shimmerinc, tresses and creamy arms and necks. They mere all beautiful at such times ; even the dowagers contrived to do with Time what Canute could not do with the waves.

But in spite of the rare vintages, the epicurian dishes, the splendid surroundings and the rapid changes, it was all slightly monotonous. They had done this sort of thing all their lives, until it had become to the actors like one of those long run and successful plays. They played at dressing, eating, drinking and flirting. They all did a little sketching. They were all accomplished ; therefore spared each other as much as possible the inflic- tion of those accomplishments. When they played or sane. they uerformed because they were asked

to do so, yet without expecting anyone to listen. This helped to kill the hours. They were votaries of pleasure who had mostl,3l lost all zest for the pleasures of life. The conver sations, brilliant although they were sometimes and refined also in their heartlessness and cynicism, were the same in that ocean saloon as took place in the drawing-rooms on shore. The softly-whispered words that they listened to on the deck, under those glorious stars, were repetitions of those insiduous whisperings they had listened to in shady conser- vatories and on river boats so often.

There was the same well-groomed uniformity amongst the men. If some were vicious they covered their criminal instinct by the same monotonous softness and politeness and lack of individuality. They all wore moustaches varying only in shade, like the colour of their morning tweeds or flannels. In the evening, however, one man might easily take the place of another without the change being observed. And the young maidens were not much more interested than were the matrons about what was whispered to them under the stars. They had their passions, doubtless, as their hostess had, yet. none of them possessed her vitality or intensity. Amongst that bevy of fair, smiling and gracious women, and their attending and courteous flatterers it is doubtful if one heart had the capability of being lit as hers was. They might be warmed, or even made red-hot by out- side flame, but they were, like asbestos, not corn- 'ssistible, and they cooled quickly. His lordship the Bishop, when be promenaded the deck with the lynx-eyed dowagers, suffered no uneasiness at what he heard or saw. His present charges had all been well trained. They would never do anything foolish or sinful. They were not impulsive, so he enjoyed himself greatly, and gradually got rid of his rheumatic and influenzic pains. The Duchess Valerie was not at all bored, if most of her guests were. She adored the ocean, and gloated on the ardent sunshine. Her love for Sir Lewis Eastlake, evil although it was, ergrossed her active mind too utterly and constantly for her to be listless. She was impatient, and wished that her wealth and fire could have thawed the ice that now separated her from the land she longed to reach. But, since that was impossible, she had the rare gift of being able to wait and enjoy what was given to her. She drank deep draughts of the fresh ocean air. She basked in the sun-heat with all the pleasure that a cat takes in a winter fire. She flattered the good Bishop and the dowagers, and divided her attentions so equally amongst her guests that no one could be jealous. Sometimes she would lend her small ears to a little flattery and honeyed words. But before the flatterer had time to flatter himself that he had made an impression her grace would turn him off with a merry jest, or sly shaft of good-tempered sarcasm. She was an adept in the wiles of the sportsmen, and not to be caught. Her male visitors knew her of old. As Sir Lewis had done, they considered her beyond their reach. The Duchess Valerie was a prude who would never commit an indiscretion or risk her place in society, and she had the ambitious pride of Queen Bess. She would reign alone unless she could conquer another kingdom with her next consort. Meantime the Gadfly glided on her journey with all the smoothness of a swallow on the wing. They put into Lisbon for two or three days, as the Duchess had a large wine-growing estate here and a comfortable château on one of the sunny hills of this pleasant city. They all enjoyed the change ashore, and were soon in the everlasting swim of fashion which is so extremely hard to enter and so difficult to keep out of, unless the votary drops out of sight altogether. They drove through the Do Chiado, the Place du Cominerce, and di Figuiera. They sauntered through the sheltered Avenue of Salitre, visited the numerous churches and convents as well as the opera house ; bought trinkets and jewellery from the goldsmiths, and did all that the Grandees expected them to do. Then satiated and fagged out with balls, receptions, and the everlasting tear and wear of their lives, they crept once more back to the comparative ease and seclusion of the Gadfly. To Madeira they next steamed, and were almost rested when they anchored once again in the hsrbour of Funchal, to be met by a host of aristocratic invalids. They were boarded by those wearied valetudinarians, and carried ashore to the fashionable hotel, where once again the same round took place. There could be no escape, this side of civilisation, for those tired-out victims of fashion.

CHAPTER XXII. TIII CYCLONE.

It is needless to say that on this ocean voyage a considerable amount of money—or its equivalents, I 0 U's—changed hands. What are fine attire and ornaments to those who have profusions of them? When the bumpkin gives to his sweetheart !I ribbon or a pair of plated earrings, he walks straight into her innermost heart, for he maketh it rejoice, and when the heart is merry it lowers its drawbridge and flings open its gates. But the patrician lover may involve himself with the Jews until he is over the ears, purchasing diamonds and other precious stones for his love, without stirring her sluggish pulse, more than just one momentary throb, if that. It is vulgar to be emotional. If his love cares for him in the patrician sense of the word love, all the gifts which Solomon bestowed upon Sheba would not one whit advance his cause. He must have the rent-roll, or the title, to make him move this well- tutored maiden. His gifts are only drops put into a full bucket. They may be pretty drops, and they are taken into the bucket, because it would be rudeness to refuse that which may impart pleasure to the giver. What are rare dishes and costly wines to those who every day fare upon them? The coster takes his Donah to the gallery of the theatre. After giving her that mental food they go to a fried fish and eel-soup establishment. If he has cash enough he will treat her to eel-soup, fried fish and potatoes, washing these delicacies down with a pot of "four" between them. He is a man in his own and in his Donah's estimation, for this generosity. She "dwells tenderly upon those even- ings for years after she has buckled into the cares of life. They are the sweet flowers of love and romance to her, the poetic reminiscences of hers and his past; the blossoms which love sent out in that spring-time before the frost. Can any fashionable young lover ever be so fatuous as to dream that his love has paid the slightest attention to the viands which helped to empty his purse, on the rare occasions that he could act the host? If they have to part, and she has to take the other fellow, it may be a certain flower that stood between them which she may remember—for a time ; never what she ate or drank. The Donah remembers for years the drama or the songs that were sung at the music-hall, while she was with her coster boy. The patrician has heard and seen too much to recollect even what particular opera it was, during which he came in to say farewell. But they are not quite blasé and emotionless, these aristocrats. They do have their moments of interests and excitements during which a strong pulse of human nature thrills through them. Wines nor spirits cannot do this, for they have supped these nectars from their boy and girl- hood. The passion of war rouses them up a little, particularly when they are hedged in by the enemy and all hope seems dead. Then the ancient instinct for fighting will grip at their languid hearts—the lust of murder, if you will—and they become rejuvenated. The savage man springs up and fills them with a strong and steadfast joy. Death sends into their nostrils his acrid breath, and they fight—fight and joy in the slaying and being slain. Next to this appetite, which makes heroes of our soldiers and fills our prisons with lower-bred criminals, comes the instinct and lust for loot. Gambling administers to, and satisfies, that passion. The excitement of the turf. From the start to the finish of a race an eternity of fierce excitement holds the man who has a stake on the result. He lives, during those few moments, a hundred weeks of his ordinary existence. On the tables at Monte Carlo, as he pitches his patrimony on the colour he patronises, he is a bunch of nervous expectation, which to him seems .eore like pleasure than anything he has been ?aged to. In the City, with shares and stocks fluctuating, lie lives with the same intensity that he does at the roulette circle, the race-course and the card- table.

That is why, now that gambling in its most exciting forms. is prohibited, these descendants of the roll-signers of Battle Abbey are rushing pell mell into the Stock Exchange. They absolutely require some excitement in order to exist. Captain Eric St. Arnoul was not a popular member of his London clubs, or rather, he had not been before his cousin, the Duchess, had intro-

dneed him to some goon otOCK excnange =Vest- ments and advanced him the money to invest. Now, however, his name was flourishing in the public papers, as director tof several bound cola. panics, and his most pressing debts all paid. Then the dowagers and the bishops had absolved him and condoned his shady past. lie was a respectable scion of the aristocracy once more. Her Grace had adNised him to be most careful in his handling of the cards and cues. He was at present on his promotion with a future before him, if he could only act on the square. He was, therefore, extremely cautious in his behaviour, and as far as he could, he acted fairly. If, therefore, owing to his extraordinary skill, a good many I 0 U's fell to his share during this voyage, he could not help that. It was out of his power to forego a game, but no man could now accuse him of pricking the cards. He had eschewed that kind of artistic work after the Calydon affair, in which his friend, Sir Lewis, had been kicked out, and he had only escaped by the skin of his teeth.

The Bishop was fond of a quiet rubber. So-were the dowagers, but as they only played for shilling points there was no possible excitement to be got out of them. He took a hand, however, when wanted with them, in order to please her Grace, but while sometimes he won, more often he lost to these ancients. So close a shave, however, did he run them, whether winning or losing, that both bishop and dowagers declared him to be a most excellent young man. It was when my lord the Bishop and the dowagers had retired to their virtuous couches that the real games commenced. Then the play ran lofty and Eric St. Arnoul fought as his ancestors had done at Hastings. He spared not, yet he played with a pure and open hand.

It is hard to overcome the habits of early youth. Often when Eric was trying to bluff his antagonist how he wished that he had that quaint little steel cockspur still in his vest pocket. Alas! he had left it behind. It is so hard to have to depend upon chance when one might be sure. Yet there was his posi- tion in the City to keep up, and her Grace, his cousin, had a remarkably sharp pair of eyes. He lost sometimes, but he won often, and, by his adroit management, kept himself popular with his cousin's guests. They all liked a clever player to tackle, and besides, they were going out to a land where they would be sure of a new excitement— that of gold mining. Gold, which fascinates the humble clerk, held this aristocratic throng in thrall. They had all brought money to purchase shares or claims in the Yukon. They were all as keen upon it as the humblest digger who had shipped for wages with the Yukon Moose Mining and Exploration Company on the river tramp that followed them from England. After enjoying the delicious climate of Madeira, and going over its spots of interest, riding or being carried in hammocks up the precipitous roads, and down into its deep green and flower-covered valleys; our voyagers next hied them to the Canaries. They paused for a day at Palmas, then once more they steamed away due west towards those fertile islands of the Spanish Main. The lofty peak of Teneriffe loomed up behind then for the greater portion of that first (iv, a marked object on the horizon. Then it faded ..nd sank gradually, and they had an unbroken circle of sea and sky. It must have been a fearsome responsibility to cross this ocean when Colombus made that first joatney of his. He believed, as the sincere advo- cate of immortality does, that there was land beyond that sky-line, and he sailed full of faith. But what anxious nights and days he must have endured, despite his faith, as the vessel plunged towards that Unknown, over those laughing blue waves! How he must have watched the log-line when it was cast over the aide to know if it drifted behind, or went with them, side by side. Each hour his men must have watched also, with boast- like terror in their eyes—the kind of terror that makes beasts grow vicious and men become murderers.

As the line floated behind, Captain Christopher was able to breathe with relief; he had gained another hour of respite from death. His faith was not yet proved a myth and a fallacy. There was no deadly current swaying them in its relentless grip and sucking them on to that fathomless abyss—the end of the earth—or if there was such a fate in store for them, they were not yet within the awful influence. - -

"It took some pluck in our forefathers to ven- ture outside the circumscribed lines of their time, my lord," observed Eric St. Arnoul to, the Bishop, as he aired his book-reading to his erudite com- panion. They were both sitting under the silken awning one forenoon, with a sun beating down upon the deck which made it decidedly warm where exposed, although the pitch had not commenced to rise yet out of the seams. It was warm, but not oppressive, and the Bishop was in a genial temper. The uric acid had been subdued for the time, at least the pains had quitted his joints. He was therefore in his most tolerant mood and answered genially: "They were terrible bigots, in those dark ages, and—well, yes, you are quite correct, captain. It did require some pluck for such adventurers as Christopher Columbus to discover America, in the days during which he lived." His lordship had won two games of cribbage from this interesting young man, who could play so well ; yet not quite well enough to beat the Bishop, on the night before. The air was balmy, although each instant becoming more sultry. "I have not felt so well for several years," remarked his lordship, daringly cracking his finger joints. "I believe I am growing younger every hour."

At this moment a sailor approached and said: "Beg pardon, sirs, but you had better go below —we are in for a buster."

"A what ? " asked the Bishop, vaguely. "A cyclone—get below as quick as you can—for by George it will be on us in another minute." The seaman rushed from the promenade deck, as if he had been a New York fireman shewing off his paces to the expectant crowd. As he did so the boatswain's whistle sounded shilly. "A rude fellow that, don't you think so, Captain St. Arnoul ? " asked the Bishop, turning to his young companion. But Eric had risen with alacrity, and without commenting on the man's 'behaviour, more rudely dragged the Bishop out of his chair. "For God's sake, quick, your lordship! Let me help you down to your cabin, or you may be washed overboard." His right reverend lordship had been airily cracking his finger joints a few minutes since; his knee joints cracked now, with the strain he put upon them as he raced along the deck towards the companion way, Eric dragging and urging him along. They were in time, but only just in time, to reach the saloon before the Bahama buster pitched upon them with its usual vehemence and speed. Fortunately for the rest of the voyagers, it Came while they were attiring for luncheon. The sailors had time to secure the openings and batten the doors.

With a devilish shriek the tornado swept upon them without warning to the uninitiated. Into the smiling blue sky it sprang with the bound and ferocity of an enraged tiger. It trailed in its rear, slate-tinted clouds and seething white waves. It laid hold of the silken awning and tore it into ribbons in one instant of time. It bent the masts like willow branches before an autumn wind. It covered the vessel three-quarters up her funnel with snowy spume, and tossed the dignified Church magnate, as if he had been a mere shuttlecock, frau one end of the saloon to the other.

A terrible moment that was to everyone on board while this sixty or a hundred-mile-per-hour wind- fiend rushed past. The agitated ocean came after this resistless infesting gust, and rose up to quash all resisting obstacles as it rushed seethingly along in the wake of the cyclone. There was consternation below during this wild half-hour of savage turmoil. Women shrieked and clung to whatever looked firm. Men set their teeth and clutched on also to rails and door handles. Heavy feet tramped overhead and hoarse voices roared through speaking-trumpets, while the wind screamed shrilly and the waves rushed over and obscured the daylight in the skylights and portholes. Then gradually peace settled down, and the pro- pellers began to work and gently thud as they had done before. The crisis was over, and they could begin to pick up their toilet apparatus, and set once again to the task of making themselves beautiful.

The Bishop picked up his episcopal frame, with the aid of his young friend St. Arnoul, with whom he had been playing skittles for the last half-hour. They both instinctively sought the electric buttons marked "brandy and soda," and pressed them fervently. Then, braced up, they began to consider where they were. "I fancy we must be nearing the Spanish Main now," observed Eric cheerfully. "This seems like Captain Kidd and Morgan's weather doesn't it, my Inrd ? "

•• WOMEN'S CHIT."

Whatever difference of opinion there may have been concerning the guilt or innocence of Dreyfus, there can only have been one feeling of sympathy with his wife in the troubles and trials which she has faced so heroinally. The pages of history and mythology furnish many stories of the devotion of women to their huebands,buts whether true or mythical, there is none more striking, ; or pathetic than that of which France has been in our day the scene. That Madame Dreyfus will break down later on however,none who know her well,teem to doubt for a moment. While there is work to do, she will do it,when there is time to rest, a collapse will probably be ! the result. Teo many siens of departing summer warn us that the best of the cycling days are over. When not ' required, it need hardly be said a bicycle is both cum- bersome, and no small trouble to keep in order during the winter, and those who make a business of stering machines are, it appears, in the habit of charging a rather heavy price for looking alter them. In Paris this difficulty has led to a curious but very convenient practice. The machine when no longer needed is simply taken to the " Pate," the headquarters of the State pawnbrokers establishments. A mere trifle is borrowed upon it at a nominal charge, and the owner of the machine goes away with a light heart, assured that not only will his or her property be quite safe, but also that it will be kept in perfect condition. Si g( neral has the system become that it is causine the State pawnbrokers no little inconvenience, but they cannot legally refuse to advance money on any machine or object that is brought to the " Monte de Pate " This felicitous idea is not of course practicable in this country. We have no system of State pawabroking, and must admit that this is one of the things they really do manage better in France. ! Mrs. Alec Tweedie contributes to the •'Queen" an emphatic warning against aver exercise on the part of ! girls. Formerly women took too lietle exercise, but now she thinks there is great danger of girls going to the ! other extreme ; and suggests a little more exercise for the mothers and a little I•ss for the daughters as the perfection which we now want to attain. Mrs. Twesdie has been looking about her, and she finds "long, thin, tired-looking girls." who . seem to be made of legs ane arms. These young people have done too much, they have no right to be as thin as they are, they have no right to stoop, the narrow chest and rounded back come from over excercise. Such is the writers view, and she holds that several of the games, such as heckey, which girls have taken up lately are too rough for women. Dr. Dukes, physician to leueby school, deals in a new book with a subject which is of importance to tsese:e parents e'no send their children away to scheot. Dur- ing the past few years, all things connected with educe, tion have improved enormously, anal no doubt the diet at hoarding sch-o's has improved also.so that in the vast majority of cases there is probably nothing to grumble about. But it was not always so, and many men and women who have reaceed middle life, have reason to remember the insuffiency of the diet when they were away at school, although they may have made no com- plaint at the time. John Wesley is said to have been half starred at the the school of his day, and thousands of lads, particularly when very young, have had similar experience. No parent ought to send a child to a board' ing sehecl where the diet is limited ,and if such a schwa is the enly one which he can afford, he would do, much better to keep the boy or girl at home. Recent articles in the " Lancet" and other papers tend tei show increased (appreciation of women as doc- tors. In recent times difficulties of no ordinary kind have beset the woman who had made up her mind to devote herself to the medical profession at home, but now her path is smoothed by:educational facilities, equal to those which are offered to men, and degrees in medi- cine are offered to her by Scottish Universities, and that of London. At the same time women who contemplate practisines as doctors must not forget that the work is laborious, and wearying, that a large sum of money has to be spent in obtaining the necessary qualifications,and that when they have been secured there is generallystor the first few years, a weary waiting for patients who show little inclination to resort to a new corner, unless be or she has exceptional degrees. One eminent prac- titioner advised a young doctor te put up hie plate and then go away for five years, but it is to be feared that even this expedient is denied, and that it is necessary for the owner of the plate to remain on the spot in order to build a reputation. Sir Walter Bement urges holiday-makers to pay more attention to the Yorkshire coast. Writing on the occa- sion of his fourth visit he says, that he "never tires of its cliffs,l its bays, its scars,eits dales, its moors, its picturesque towrs, and above all its kindly people, who, if you approach them in a proper spirit, will be found to be surpassed by none, in their kindliness and civility." Low' 11 loved Whitby above all other seaside places. Du Nieurier went t the same town year after year; and Sir Walter, himself is enthusiastic concerning the charms of Robin Hood's Bay. His counsel may be commended to those people in the Midland towns, verging towards the North, who either go to Skegness, or undertake a tiring journey to the Isle of Man. " Mama, how deep is the sea ?" is a question which has been heard by a good many people during the past three months. and Mama in most cases has answered discreetly and warily, that the depth varies from a few feet to a much greater distence. A few days ago Sir John Murray who was on board H.M.S. " Challenger " during her historic cruhe quoted to the British Asso- ciation some interesting statistics on this subject, Of the 103 million square miles which the ecsan covers, more than half is at the depth of between two and three milee. The greatest depth is at a spot in the South Pacific!, where the " Challenger "found the bottom to he more than five miles away. Below two miles all is darkness, and the temperature is only about 85 degrees. In hats, many of the newest shapes closely resemble the Salvation Army "poke," with flaring brims which stand up from the face, and diminish to a mere nothing at the back. Some are cut out at the back, and the majority are raised a little at the side. The jam pot crown appears to be coming in again, and the Puritan shape is prettily adopted to Dame Fashioe's require- ments. The sequin, again this autumn shows no sign of diminishing in favour. For four seasons now it has twinkled its merry way on our gowns, wrap% and head- geareind there seems every probability of the craze con- tinuing yet another four. The sever sequin it is rumoured is to have the measure of our best affections, but at present this seems somewhat doubtful. " MADGE."

INFECTIOUS DISEASES HOSPITAL. THE PROPOSED EXTENSION.

STRONG OPPOSITION IN WHITECROSS WARD.

An agitation is being conducted in the Whitecross Ward against the proposed extension of the infee- tious diseases hospital, and a petition against the project has been forwarded to the Local Govern- ment Board. At the Town Council meeting in August, plans were laid on the table of the proposed extensions, and they were unanimously passed without comment. Permission to purchase land, and carry out the extensions was given in the Cor- poration Bill. The plans have now been forwarded to London and await the sanction of the Local Government Board. The presetnt hospital has served its purpose for over twenty years, and the continued growth of the town has rendered the extensions necessary. At the present moment the accommodation is taxed to the utmost, and still there are numbers of infectious cases being treated at hom-,, the authorities being unable to receive them at the hospital. The Health Committee has decided to proceed with the extensions at the earlieA possible moment, and the scheme they hese decided upon is of an expensive character,involving the spending of something like £5,000 or ..£6,000. A portion of the land required has been bought and negotiations are proceeding for the remainder. The most noticeable feature of the intended alter- ations is the provision of a new entrance at Goulden-street. This will necessitate the patients being conveyed along Lovely-lane and thence down Goulden-street, and such a procedure is strongly objected to by the residents and property owners. It is intended to erect an entirely new administrativt, block in close proximity to the proposed entrance, and in addition to a new ward, the present administra- tive building, which is too small for present day requirements, will also be coeverted into a fever ward. It is also intended in the present scheme to still keep the lodge and gates in use in Aikin-street but to reserve them exclusively for the departure of patients. All the patients will be ad- mitted by the Goulden-street entrance.

'This scheme has caused a great deal of comment in the district concerned dating the past week and i a meeting has been held to consider the situation. The following petition has also been prepared with the object of submitting it to the Local Govern- ment Board :—" To the President of the Local Government Board. The humble petition of the undersigned residents of the neighbour- hood of Lovely-lane, Golden-street, Monks- street, and Collin-street showeth that the Corporation of Warrington propose extend- ing the fever hospital and making a new entrance out of Goulden-street. The locality con- tains a large number of better-class houses which will be seriously depreciated in value, and the very mention of the scheme has created quite a scare in the neighbourhood. Your petitioners are of opinion that the present access is ample for all purposes and that it is wholly unnecessary to have a second entrance and your petitioners therefore pray that the plans now submitted to you by the Corporation may be amended s 3 as to save the necessity for this second entrance and your petititioners will ever pray." etc: This petition, it may be stated, has al- ready been signed by 27 property owners in the im- mediate neighbourhood and 50 residents. From information which a reporter of the " Examiner " has been able to gather from the opponents to the scheme, the opinion prevails that as an alternative scheme to the one suggested, the present administrative block might be enlarged by the addition of another storey, and thus avoid the necessity of a second entrance leading to a new ad- ministrative building. Failing this, the opponents to the extensions object tothe expenditure of a sum of over £5,000 on a site which is admittedly an unsuitable onefcr an infectious diseases hospital. The present building is surrounded by residents, and in close proximity to large works. It is argued that if such a large expenditure is necessary, it would be better to build an entirely new hospital outside the borough. We understand that the health Committee will be asked to receive a depu- tation on the sulject at its next meeting.

THE LATE MR. J. E WORSLEY.

THE FUNERAL.

The grave closed over the remains of the deceased gentleman on Friday morning, when all that ie,mained mortal was interred in the family grave situated on the South-Rasterly side of Winwick Churchyard. The hour fixed for the funeral service was ten o'clock,and promptly at the time appointed the Rev. Canon Penrhyn (rector), and the Rev. J. W. Williams (cunste) commenced the service in the Church. The coffin was covered with beautiful wreaths,and lay in the chancel whither it had been borne earlier in the morning by the bell-ringers of the Church; The funeral was of a semi-private character, and notwithstanding the early hour of the service, a fairly-large number of people atten- ded. The relatives present were :—Mrs. Worsley (widow), Mr. Edward and Mr. Hugh Worsley (sons), Miss Eleanor, Miss Mary, Miss Agnes and Miss Joyce Vvorsley (daughters), Mr and Mrs Rigg of Golborne. Amongst the friends and sympathisers present were: Mr C B F Thrron, J.P., C.C., Mr T Ormond, Mr Collingwood, Mr E T Evans, and Mr shirley (clerk) on behalf of the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council; Rev R Smith (rector of Lowton),Dr White, Mr John White, J.P., Mr Herbert Woods (Messrs Ashton and Woods), Mr Taylor (Messrs Greenall and Buckton), Mr Mullard (clerk to the Lymm Urban District Council), Mr Dobson (deputy coroner to the deceasel). Mr Granger, Mr Peter Longton), rector's warden of Win wick Church),Mr Massey (sidesman) Mr Pennington, of Woodhead,. Mr Shaw, Mr Clarke (schoolmaster at Winwick), Mr Thomas Warburtors, and others.

The service in the church having been concluded, the ceremony at the graveside was brief. The Rev Canon Penrhyn officiating. 'i he coffin was lowered into its last resting place by the church bell-ringers namely, Messrs F Bridge, W Bridge, T Edgington,R Edgington, Arthur Baker, and Albert Vardy. The inscription on the coffin was simply " James Edwardson W0n1ey,1899." The grave had been lined with evergreens, and numerous floral tributes had been sent. At the close of the funeral a muffled peal was rung.

REFERENCE AT NEWTON SESSIONS. Before the commencement of the ordinary busi- ness at the Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday morning, the Chairman (Captain Withingtony said that he wished to express on behalf if himself and his brother magistrates their feelings of 103E3 sus- tained by the death of a solicitor on the previous day, who had practised most courteously in that court ;he referred to Mr. Worsley. Be thought an intimation of their sorrow should be sent to de- ceased's relatives.

Mr. J. W. Knowles, on behalf of the solicitors practising at the court, said that they deeply sym- pathibed with the family in the loss they had sus- tained. He was sure tha., they and the public of the district would feel very much grieved at the loss of Mr. Worsley at such a comparatigely young age.

A VOTE OF CONDOLENCE. At a special meeting of the Urban District Council of Newton-in-Makerfield, held on Moneay the 18th inst. the Chairman (Mr. Borron) hi moving a vote of condo- lence with Mrs 'Worsley and family in their loss sus- tained by the de itu of Mr. Worsley expressed the sorrow with which he and every member of the Council had heard of the loss they as a rublic bociv, had stained by his death, and moved the following r,sclution, viz:— Resolved. " That this Council havinf, heard with deep regret of the death of James Edwardwon Worley, Egg., of Winwick, for over 20 years solicitor and legal adviser to this authority, desires to express its appreciation and recognition of the valuable services rendered by Mr. Worsley, and to place upon record its deep regret at the great loss the Council have sustained by his death, and that a copy of this resolution be forwarded to Mrs Wors- ley, as a respectful expression to her and her sorrowing family of the sympathy and condolence of every mem- ber of this C )uncil in their sad bereavement "—This was supported by Mr. Ormand and Mr. Gilmour, and car- ried in silence.

PULPIT REFERENCES. On Sunday. the Rev. Canon Penrhyn, rector and rural dean, preached a very impressive, sermon in the morn- ing at St. Oswald'ri Parish Church, Winwick, in which he made sub able reference to the late Mr. Worley. He spoke of his excellent character, and his connection with that church for over 20 years, as parishioner's warden for 12 years, during which time he had rendered valuable s(Tvices to the church and parish. They would miss him very much. The Beetlr also alluded to the other offices which the deceased had held, as coron:r for the Honour and Fee of Halton, law clerk to the Newton Urban District Council, Lao., the duties of which he dis- charged with great credit to himself and those around him. He was a kind husband and father, and Mrs. Worsley and his family had their deepest sympathy in their sad bereav, meet. In the afternoon the Rev. W. Williams, curate, also alluded in a very effecting manner to the deceas, d gentleman.

WARRINGTON CHAMBER Op CONIMERCR.

The monthly meeting of the Council of this Chamber was held at the Market-gate Chambers on Monday. Alderman Roberts in the chair. The Secretary (Councillor Bennett) reported that he had arranged with the Board of Trade to exhibit in Warrington, at an early date, the whole of the coin- mercial samples collected by Mr. T. Worthington during his special mission to South America. Some discussion took place as to the desirability of arranging for a public lecture on the resources of Western Australia. and it was decided to consider the matter fully at the next meeting. Mention was made of the movement to boycott the Paris Exhibi- tion as a protest against the recent verdict in the Dreyfus case, but although the feeling of indigna- tion at what was generally regarded as a gross per- version ot justice was very pronounced, it was not thought desirable for the Chamber to take any official action in the matter. Mr. Edward Garnett, who attended the autumnal meeting of the Associa- ted Chambers at Belfast, communicated his impres- sions of the proceedings.

NEWS OF VIE GAME.

THE PARIS EXHIBITION TOURNEY.—The proposed international tourney at Polish Draughts to be held

at Paris during the Exhibition a 1900 is nom assured, we learn from" La Tribune des Damistes,' a generous amateur having promised the sum of 500 francs towards the prize ft lid. Many other subscriptions are also promised,a%d subventions are anticipated from the President of the Republic lied from the Municipal Council of Paris. The Parisian players, headed by Moryzieur Leclercq, editor of the monthly magazine " Le Jeu de Dim, s," and ably assisted by Messieurs Barathon,Baudry, Chardonnet and Dussaut, have taken the matter up with spirit, and are forming an association t) be called "Le Cercle du Damier Parieien," under who,e auspices the tourney will be carried out. Arrangements will be made for a special tariff at the hotels during the tourney for the conte-Aants and all draughts-play- ing visitors. It is intended to hold the tourney at the Cafe du Globe. Boulevard de Strasbourg, Paris during Whit week. The entrance fee for com- petitors is fixed at 25 francs (about .E 1 There will be three sittings each day during the tourney, and a charge for admission will be made to spectators of 2 francs for the day. or 1 franc for each sitting or of 10 francs for a season ticket for the whole duration of the tourney. All entrants to the tourney and also all subscribers of 10 francs to the funds, will be entitled to free admission. The value and number of the prizes is not yet announced. and will of course, depend in a great measure upon the amount of the funds subscribed.

The figures thus (11-15) indicate that a move is me de from and to th squares so numbered. Black has the opening move and the pairs of figures apply alternately to Black and White to the close.

BOILERMAKER'S ADVICE TO THE

QUEEN.

CURIOUS LIVERPOOL INCIDENT'. A small sensation has been caused in the north part of Liverpool by the new-born enthu-ism in certain directions of an old boiler-maker named William Price. Mr. Price, a man of 59 years, lives in Ethiopia-street, off Foley-street, Kirkdale. A shrewd and welcspoken man, he is immensely popular with his wsrkmates and the people of his district. Be has worked hard at his trade for many years, and his keen kindly face with its twinkling eyes nder the grey brows is as well known among the people as the toseer of the old c'aurch not far from his residence. He is a most intelli- gent and interesting character, with a sturdy inde- pendence which (as a neighbour remarked) would make him just as ready to offer his opinion to the Queen as to yourself—the reporter wno was looking for him. And so, curiously enoue.h. it proved. for Mr. Price used precisely the same expression :—" I would say the same to Queen V eerie," he said, by way of confirming something which (when presently discovered by our representative) he had ben descrih- in.'. During the last few months Mr. Price has for some time been missed from his circle. The b )iler-meking yard has bee% without him, and his mates have inquired often and anxiously as to the reason of his absence. Re- cently he has again been up and doing, with apparently a new lease of life and vigour. Far from being loqua- cious before, he now talks with great freedom, but al- ways on one subject. The Transvaal, Royal visits, Australian cricketers, are waved aside, and all his elo- quence devoted to a single subject, which has taken possession of his mind entirely and completely. A news- paper representative had a talk with this intsresting character the other day, with the idea of learning all about it. " First and foremost, Mr. Price, said the writer, " are you willing to see in print everythiug you tell me to day ?" "Every word, my son.' I'm going to tell you nothinv.but Gospel truth, and if you print it in every paper in England, all the better." " Very well ; to start with, what took you away from the boiler-yard ?" "See, here !" and the man turned up his jacket sleeve, and, rolling up his shirt, disclosed a brawny arm which was slightly blotched here and there with rd spots. " A little while ago." he said in explanation of this phenomenon, " a rash came on that hand. It spread up the arm, and the arm began to swell. I saw a doctor and he said it was eczema. He gave me stuff to rub it with, and I rubbed it, but the arm swelled up worse than ever. It grew a terrible size. I don't believe there's a man in Liverpool had an arm as big as that was. I kept on using the doctor's stuff, but presently my leg started, first with a rash and then a swelling, just like the arms. I saw another doctor, but still failed to obt tin relief. I got so had that I couldn't sleep at nights. My arms and I.gs itched terribly, and my legs were FO much swelled I could do nothing. I rubbed them with sulphur ointment until the akin came off. I tried first this remedy and then thee, but nothing did me any good, and I thought I should never work again. Then my daughter here saw an account in the 'Mercury' of some of the cures effected in Livernool by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. Well. I didn't at first attach to them as much importance As I should, but a friend of my daughter said they would do what nothing else would do—ltad cured cases which all the doctors had given up. So she persuaded me to try a box. do thee no harm,' she said to me 'and it might do good.' She got a box for nte,and before I Wfla throueh`with it I already felt better" "How did the improvement show itself ?" "The swelling went down and I slept better. I kept taking Dr. Williams' pink pills and I got better all the time. It a.tenished everybody. After four boxes I was a different man. I've now had eight, and am thankful to say I am well enough to start work on Mon- day morning, for the first time during a long pried." Here the old man slapped the arms of his chair an i launched out into praises of Dr. Williams' pink pills. " There's nothing like them." he said. "they'll de what nothing elce will. I'd recommend those pills to Queen V,ctoria herself, and you can put that in the paper."

11-1 E EXAMINER, FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 22, 1899. THE HOUSE OF LORDS. HARVEST FESTIVALS. BY AI7GUSTINE BIRRELL, Q.C., M.P. THE PROFFERED SERVICES OF WARRINUTON VOLUNTEERS.

A. progressive party is always in the early stage of its progress at a dialectical disadvantages with those who are content to say with Lord Mel- bourne: " Way cannot you let it alone?" Exist- ing Institutions by the mere fact of their ability to go on existing are hard to attack, particularly when (as is u-ually the case) their critics are not ab,o- lately at one as to what (if anything) is to be put in their place. But this notwithstanding, a cause is often a lost, cause long before its supporters have ceased chuckling over the dissensions in the enemies' camp. The House of Lords may live a long time, but it is a lost cause. It is not broad-based upon anything Nobody can say of it that it is a consultative, deli- berative authoritative Second Chamber. To go and look at it is enough. In Finance it has nothing to say. The great majority of its members take no part in its proceedings, and if they did, nothing would be added to the dignity of Parliament. The House of L )rds contains eminent men. risen from the ranks ; but they were not sent there for the purposes of legislation, but for reasons of a compli- mentary character, and knowing the unreality of the whole thing they are dumb. Other men who are not. eminent are sent there for reasons with which the whole country has grown contemp- tuously familiar. Second Chamber men, as some political critics are now called, cannot say that the House of Lords plays the part usually assigned by constitutional writers to a Second Chamber. What, then, is the function of the lionse of Lords at the present period of the development of the British Constitution? What is its place in the providential order of our political cosmos? Mr. Balfour, who ought to know, told us in the course of an interesting speech he made in the House of Commons on the 13th of February, 1899, what he takes this to be. It is " the power of rejecting a measure and of going on rejecting it," until the constituencies have been consulted at a General Election. Only this and nothing more! If the constituencies at a General Election give a majority in the House of Commons to the party which has included the obnoxious measure within its general scheme of legislative reforms, then the House of Lords must succumb to the odd man, give up its opposition. and at least allow the Bill a second rea4ing. How their lordships are to behave in the Committee Stage, Mr. Balfour, who scorns detail, does not tell as, and yet before now a Bill has been killed in Committee.

The Whole force of this argument turns upon the character of what I have called the ob- noxious measure. If it is of a revolulon- ary nature, and has been sprung upon a nation of football players and cricketers by an imperious minister, all will agree that the constituencies ought to have their say upon it, but can it be necessary to maintain the Louse of Lords on its present footing in order to secure so rudimentary a right to the Wectorate ? What is a Revolutionary Measure? At present it is a matter of the imagination. A publican and a parson would probably respectively be of the opinion that Local Veto and Disestablishment are revolutionary measures. Was the Irish Local Government Act a revolutionary measure? It has certainly effected a revolution. Yet the Constitu- encies were not consulted about it. Was Free lidu- caAon a revolutionary measure? Lord Salisbury did not stop (being in a great hurry) to ask the opinion of the Constituencies.: Was the Workmen's Compensation Act a revolutionary measure? It certainly introduced a novel princi- ple of the most far-reaching character into every workshop and building yard in:the country, which none the less knew nothing about it till it became law. Would payment of members be revo:ution- ary ? UnleF.:s by some pedantic construction,it was laid down that nothing is revolutionary that does not directly concern the constitutional relations of the Crown, the Lords and the Commons one to another, nobody is in a position to say at the date of its introduction who her a measure is revolu- tionary or not. Consequently it follows that the House of Lords, in Mr. Balfour' s opinion,is to be at liberty to reject, and to go on rejecting, every measure that is intro- duced to them, until in 80/10 mysterious way it is brought home to their minds that the constituencies disapprove of their conduct. This is preposterous enough but does it exercise this right? Certainly not. It is a permanent Conservative body, who, however much it may hate a great deal of Lord Salisbury s legislation hates Liberal legislation much more, and, therefore, in order to keep Lord Salisbury in and the Liberals outs it meekly drains the chalice which Lord Salisbury holds to its lips, but spurns it when proffered by the twenty or thirty peers who it on the other side of the House. When the Tories are in office the country has no Second Chamber. When the Liberals are in office we have a Second Chamber existing for the purpose of obstructing and mutilating Liberal Legislation in order to make it plain to the country that if any- thing is to be done at all only the Tories can do it. No severer criticisze of a Constitution can be uttered than to be able truthfully to say of it, that it conserves, and only conserves the political in- terests of one of the two historical parties of the State.

To appeal to the constituencies every time the House of Lords kills or renders futile a piece of Liberal legislation is impossible. Mr. Balfour thinks that a delay of twenty years or so is no great mat- ter. But then Mr. Balfour has never greatly de- sired any legislative reform. He was born into a world ready-furnished for him. What does he know I of the hope deferred that makes the heart sick? Says he," and whereas the House of Lords have I done and will do great service in rejecting Bills which never reappear, they have done not less ser- vice, believe me, in delaying fer five, ten, or fifteen years, a measure tor which the country was not ripe, for which the country had made no emphatic demand, and the delay in carrying out which has prevented those violent reections the absence of which is the most distieguishing mark of Engiish history." What does it matter to Mr. Balfour that four generations of young men were denied access to the Universities of their native land by t4e crass stupidity of the House of Lords? I suppsse the country was not ripe for this measure,or wss it that the demand made for it was not sufficiently emphatic? "Believe ma" says Mr. Balfour, for- getting that no politican who is in earnest will ever believe him. _

Between the Liberal Party and the House of Lords there must now always be active hostility. it is no longer possible to eoncieve a Liberal Ministry doing itself justice and fully discharging its duties as an instrument of government whilst the House of Lords remains in possession of its existing powers. Those powers must be curtailed if the Liberal Party is ever again usefully to com- pete for power with its opponents. Mr. Gladstone foresaw this and in ths very last speech he ever made in the House of Commons declared that a solution would have to be found "for this tremend- ous contrariety, and incessant conflict upon matters of high principle sisd profound importance between the representatives of the people and tho-e who till a nominated Crlamber." He might have added that it is not only on matters of high principle and pro- found importance that the Rouse of Lords enters into 'conflict with the representatives of the people. In small matters as well as in largo, in things administrative, as well as in things legislative, in County business as well as in National, does the House of Lords set, itself the task of undermining the authority of Liberal Governments, curbing their power and destroying their utility. THE VETO OF THE LORDS MUST BE REMOVED. It is no light task. It will require a bold leader and a favourable hour, but the chance! are that we shall not have to wait quite so long as Lord Salis- bury seems to imagine, in the meantime it is some- thing to know that the whole Liberal Party throughout the country is now convinced, the most cautious no leas than the most enthusisstic,that the present situation is intolerable and that unless ths Lords are deprived of their veto the Liberalism of , the future must remain an empty dream.

CARTER'S LITTLE TR PILL!

REFERENCE BY TEIE REV. JOHN Y NGE. Harvest thanksgiving services were conducted in Wycliffe Congregational Church, on Sunday, morn- ing and evening, by the Rev. John Yonge. A choice collection ot plants and flowers were displayed on the communion table and within the rails. At the morning service the preacher based his remarks on the text "Let us not be weary in well doing, for in due season we shall reap, if we faint not,' and spoke of the encouragement, to persevere in all spiritual labour derived from the certainty of even- tual harvest. There was a vary large congregation at the evening service. The choir very efficiently sang two anthems " Thou visitest the Earth 't (Barbly) and" Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem" (E. V. Hall). Mr. Arthur Griffiths sang the solo part in the last-named anthem. Collections were taken on behalf of the Church funds and realised £l5. The Rev. John Yonge based his discourse on the text "And the Seventh Angel sonuded, and there were great voices in heaven, saying, the kingdom of this world are become the kingdom of our Lord and His Chrisc, and He shall reign for ever and ever" Reve- lation xi, 15v. In the course of his remarks he said the Christian had no right to become a pessi- mist and just in proportion as he felt the tendency coming upon him was he bound to face and fight it until the better faith should conquer. He pointecl out the circumstances under which John saw the vision in the isle of Patmos, and stated that since then close upon 1900 years had rolled away. They were approaching now the dawn ot the 20th cen- tury, so that they were by that much the " NEARER, THE DIVINE EVENT" of which John testified. They had at least the knowledge of the general fact that to-day the knowledge of the true God was spreading over the face of the earth. Some of the many forms of idol worship had already been utterly abolished. Ancient heathenisms were slowly giving way to the undermining processes of Christian civilisation and education—specially in India and also in a swiftly accelerating degree, even in China. Never was the gospel of Christ so widely proclaimed as now. But while all this was most glori- ously true, still probably as they looked at the text and , then looked out upon.life and the world, they were al- most tempted to ask in despondent tone, and " when shall these things be?" They found themselves faced by many difficulties ; many signs of evil, which some- times seemed to preponderate over the signs of good. Not alone did the fact yet exist that the majority of the race was still unsubdued by the gospel but even in lands and nations professedly bearing tee name of Christian— the truths of Christ, the principles of Christ, the spirit of Christ hut not gained anything like supremacy.

THE DIVISIONS IN TEIE CHURCH. ' The Church that acknowledged him, as Lord and King was tenthly torn by divisions and separations ; it was -yet held in the thrall cf manifold supersti- tion ;it was still the arena of c3nfliet and con- troversy; in some of its most prominent sections it was more concerned about material and external trifles than about spiritual aims and responsibilities. It was more bent upon being c3mfortable and outwardly prosperous, than upon being self-sacrificing and devoted to the wel- fare of men. The church was eating for herself far more than for her L3rd, or than for the souls for whom He died.

SOCIAL PROBLEMS. Then again human society, even in Christian countries was still marked and disfigured by inequalities,injustices and oppressions. The spectacle was still presented of bloated wealth at one end of the community and squalid poverty at the other. Men were daily: mounting to riches, extracted often from the lives of ill-paid, ill-clad. and ill-housed workers ; the poor were living wretched lives and even if they had the gospel preached to them, learnt only too bitterly that their inheritance was all future and none presPnt. Vice enslaved mult;tild( s. The public press of Christian countries detailed the prurient scandals of so-called " society." The public streets of Christian cities witnessed unblushing immorality and besotted or blatant drunkenness. This Christian age was called to revel in the revival of brutal Ind degrading sports, while the vast majority of their populations misused the Sabbath,deserted the sanctuary derided the Holy Book, and defamed the Sacred Name. These conditions gave seeming centradiction to the splendid hopes and prophecies of the gospel. SABO of the social problems were turning even religious men sceptical and cynical. Fie confessed that he did not know of any method whereby to study these matters safely apart from the faith which clung to the eternal righteousness of the Almighty. Every no cv and then did they not get tokens, signs clear, if fleeting, that the cause of Gad and Christ was making progress. They need have no hesitancy in taking as samples two recent event,' which had struck the attention of the w)rld. First of all, there was

THE RECENT PEACE CONFERENCE.

A century, even half a century ago, such an event as that would have beers absolutely utopian. Maly p-ris'e deemed it to b 2 so now and withhell their synspatny from it as hopeless, and doomed from the first. The re- sult had proved that it was neither one nor the other. The fact remained that the close of the 19tn century had actually witnessed a "Parliament of war." All nations and peoples and tongues, practically by their chosen representatives hal canferre3 in the interests of universal peace. They had not secured the abolition of wars or even the diminution of. armaments, but they had advanced the interests of peace immeasurably. They had brought International arbitration into the realm of practical politics, and the mere fact of their having conferred together was verily a " tremendous stride " in the right direction. It had gone a long way to help to the formation of a public opinion in the countries favourable to peace and adverse to war. To what were they to assign the occurrence of that significant and far reaching event ? Were they to assign it cynically t s the wish of the Czar to further some secret Imperial design or were they to believe that he in suggesting it, and the Government of the world in falling in with it had been unconsciously perhaps, but really moving under the influences and guidance of the King of Kings, who was the " Prince of Peace ?" Christian faith, at any rate, might be forgiven its audacity if it dared to believe that in and behind it all, He had been wotking, who yet shouli have the Kingdom for his own. TIIE DIFFICULTY IN TRH TRANSVAAL.

It cam;;;;;;r10-ui-;n-ci. questionable sequel to the Peace Conference that our nation—s) conspicuous and influential a partaker in its doings—should seem t ) be immediately afterwards in danger of drifting into strife. The Transvaal question was one that was beset by many difficulties, and upon it good and till men of light and leading seemed to be at perplexing variance. S-eing, however, that the contrwersy existed bet oreen a small republic, largely composed of peasant farmer., and the most powerful empire that was concernei in Af ic4, it did seem a. if peaceable settlement should be c,rtain. One was disposed to think it would be certain but for the military passion that was being so seduloucly engen- dered. If war ensued it would be largely because an irresponshle press at home and in South AFrica, and mercenary motives, military aggressiveness and thirst for Imperial extension, had misled and do ninated the country. If war ensued it would be a needless war, and therefore an unjust war, and, clnsequently, a war that could only involve eventual disaster on the nation that unri2htelusly enga,ged in it. Is seemed to him that their Christian duty WSS to protest against this war and against the spirit of militarism that w is so much a': the back of it.

THR C&LL TO VOLUNTEY.RS. That it should have seemed nec-s.ary to the Warring- ton Volunteer officers to call for personal off.srs of ser- vice in this war from their fellow-townsmen vras a matter much to be deprecat-d. The deed had al air of unreality, of absurdity, of puerility, of mock heroics ahout it. But it gave only one more unhappy evidence that an unhealthy snirit of amhitious Ache was assert- ing itself, which boded no good to our mithn. No one would impugn the courage even of volunteers if any real occasion called for its display, but on 5,4C11 an °lca- sion as this one d'id feel disposed to qu-stion alike the good sense and worthy spirit, both of officers and men. Earnestly, however, must they hope and pray that, even rms.:o this "war might be avertel. It would he a dis- honour to Eogland to rush from a Peace Conference to destrly a free republic, a gimp Statl standing tin for i's independence, but which with firmness and patience oo our side could he led to se!tle this clispt)te in peace, Might God save our country from this unchriAtian strife.

evil as it still might be, than wh.n John took his view of the future and saw the victory of his Lord ! Jesus was winning, it might be slowly, but most surely it was certainly. The tread of events was all towards the triumph of Divine purpose. The Divine husbandman had been busy through the ages {sowing., nurturing, and developing" the seed of the Kingdom," and he never "laboured in vain, nor spent His strenght for nought. ' God's great harvest was infallibly coming on. Every harvest field proclaime I it. Every Harvest Festival should ring with t:,.e assurance. Then,let them believe in it ! let them pray for it ! let theca work for it ! let them hold back no effort and grudge no gift or sacrifice in a cause so splendidjor an issue and result so transcendent.

THE WORLD WAS BETTER TO-DAY

Sr. MARY'S, GREIT SANKEY.

The annual narvest thanksgiving services in connec- tion with the above church were held on Sunday last The church, as in former years, had been lavishly deco- rated, and the distribution of the flowers,cern,and fruit, was indicative of extreme good taste, the whole effect beieg very beautiful. The morning service was very well attended, every available seat being occupied. The clergy present were Rev. E. S. Jackson, vicar; Rev J.R. Jones, curate ; and the Rev. H. Gorse. The service opened with the usual hymn "Come ye thankful people come," and suitable hymns were used throughout the service. The ftev. Canon G. H. Spooner, M.A., Rural Dean of Claildwall, preached the sermon, taking for his theme the text "First the blade, then the ear, then the full corn ie the ear." An anthem, "The Lord is loving unto His peop'e," was rendered by the choir, the solo be- ing taken by Miss M. Ray. Holy Cominunion was held after the service. In the afternoon, a children's flower service was held at 3 o'clock, the children bringing their offerings of flowers, which were afterwards conveyed to to the Warrington Infirmary. This service was also very well attended, the children of course being in the majority. The Rev. H. Gorse, 8.A., preached a very plain and simp!e sermon to the children and drew some very pretty lessons from the objects of the service. The evening service, however, was the one in which chief interest was centred, reputation of the decorations drawing many strangers. The church was packed, in fact many more would have gone in, but owing to every available ineh of space being occupied they were obliged to stay outside. The preacher at this service was the Rev. Joseph Holden, vicar of Antrobus, who preached a very able sermon from the text " He shall save both man and beast." The choir rendered the anthem" Theyeshall go up with joy" with good effect. The Revs. J. R. Jones and H. Gorse were also present. the Vicar being unab!e to be present at the evening service, owing to the rough weather. After service the congregation walked round the church to view the decorations, and these evoked many expres- sions of admiration. Collections were taken ac each service, the total amount being just under £27, which considering the huge, congregations is rather disappoint- ing, and much less than last year's takings.

WARRINGTON CJNaNCTITION scaooL.

DISTRIBUTION OF PRIZES.

At People's College School on Monday evening, the Continuation Classes in connection with the War- rington Technical Institute were re-commenced. The annual distribution of prizes took place. In the absence of Councillor Monks, Chairman of the Institute Sub- COMMittee, Mr. Charles Madeley (secretary), presided. There was a fairly large attendance of students.

===== 0002601_18990922_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ANNUAL REPORT.

The SECRETARY read his annual report, which was as follows :—" The number of pupils on the register of the Continuation School last session was 239. In this res- pect we have to report a decrease as compared with the previous year of about 69. Tnie decrease is probably attributable to the success of the evening schools which have been opened in other parts of the town, and which are now fairly established ; and as it was accompanied by a very considerable increase in the higher commer- cial classes, we do not consider that we have nauch to complain of. The number of class entries was as follows :—English composition and writing, 243 ; ari- thmetic, 233 ; shorthand, 115 ; drawing, 83 ; chemistry 45 ; needlework, 37 ; cookery, 36. These figures corn, pare favourably with those of last year, as they indicate that pupils have on the average entered for a greater number of subjects tlaan before, which is in itself a good thing. And, as the increase in subject entries was ac- companii el by a steadier average attendance, which means more regular work, it follows as a matter of course that we have proportionately a larger number of attendance prizes to distribute. The utvnber this year is 97. The examinations show a similar result. Thsro were not so many candidates, but we had very few failures, only 13 out of 88 who sat,and we obtained more passes in proportion to our numbers, and those of a higher aver- age grade, than last year. On the passes thus gained the following scholarships, under the usual conditions, have been awarded :—John Critchley and Arthur Pennine-.ton, scholarships value 7s 6i ; G G Dawson, Frederick Fou kes, James Knott.and Frederick Mellor, scholarships value 53. In addition, free admission to the School of Art for the present session has been awarded to the following four pupils who did the best papers at the examination in drawing at the end of the session : Rtanley Houghton, Herbert Molyneaux,Willism Knowles Sydney Massey. Six lantera lectures were delivered by Mr Hall, as usual, at intervals during the session, and the committee is also indebted to Mr. Greening and Mr. E. E. Lowe, for delimit:lg interesting lectures upon subjects of Natural History, which formed an agreeable change from our usual course. In conclusion. the corn • mittee desires to acknowledge cordially the efforts made by Mr. Hall and other teachers to promote the suceess of the school. The repot': was adopted.
