THE EARTH'S MAGNETISM. LECTU RE AT THE WA.R-R IS GTON MUSEN M.

Mr. J. R. Ashworth, M.S., F.P.S., delivered a deeply interesting lecture in conneetion with the Warrington Technical Institute on Monday evening. It was given in the lecture room of the Museum, and there was a ' good attendance. The lesture was illustraten by lantern views, diagrams, and experiments. Mr. Lowe, of th Museum, manipulated the lantern. At the outset Mr. Asavrorern dealt with the elemee- tary facts about the magnet, and said he supposed they were all acquainte 1 with the fact that the needle wauld point in one definite direction. In this peert of the world it was very nearly north and south. He wished them to notice that the needle was very powerfully affected by any neighbouring magnet. He illustrated with a bar magnet the susceptibility of the needle to move iato whatever position the bar was placed, always standing approximately in a line with it. Tills experi- ment suggested to Dr. Gilbert, of Colehester, the idea that the whole of the earth might be one huge magnet, and that idea had been confirmed to a great extent in the course of tine. The lecturer showed a rough model of the earth, iaside of which was a magnetized bar of iron running from one extremity of the model to the other. By an experiment the lecturer demonstrated how the meguetised bar effected the magnetic needle, and compelled it to paint in the direction of the bar. The needle in Eagdand poin- ted to the west of true north. Ia America it pointed true north. In the Pacific Oeeau it began to deviate in the opposite direction. That continued right across. Asia and Chins till they came to a corresponding posi- tion to North America on the other side of the world. That illustrated the distribution of magnetism over the surface of the earth. A very interestiee question arose. In the first place it was quite certain that the eerth had not got a large magnet running through it. His experi- ment was only a rough way of illustrating what went on. Where then did this magnetism of the earth reside ? Did it reside in the atmosphere, the crust, or the interior of the earth ? That was rather difficult to answer. It was almost certain it did not reside in the atmosphere. The opinion prevailed that it resided in the. crust of the earth, and did not penetrate very deeply, but only perhaps to the extent of a few milis They were not, of course, quite certain about that The reason it would not penetrate very deeply,however, was because whenever any substance got into a white heat it lost all magnetism and remained So until it be- came cold. As they knew that the interior of the earth was practically at a white hest they could hardly sup- pose that it had any magnetism about it. By an experi- ment, the lecturer showed how heat disturbed maenetic qualitiee. He then directed attention to the changes which had gone on in the state of the earth's magne- tism in the course of time. They might ask whether the magnetic needle always stood like it was in England at the present time ? It now stosci about 16 degrees west of north. It had not always done GO. It had changed in a very peculiar and systematic manner. In 1580,the earliest observation ever made of the position of the magnetic needle, it stood on the easterly side of North. In 16b7 it stood absolutely true north and south. le the course of 77 years it had changed fro n an easterly position to true north and south. In another 70 years it stood westerly about as much as it had previously been easterly. In 1800 it had reached a considerable de- viation to the west, 24 degrees, the largest westerly de- viation ever noticed. Since 1800 to the present time the deviation had been slightly decreasing. They would see that there was something systematic about the devia- tion. He illustrated be means of a diagram the systematic movement of the needle, and pointed out that in the future they would again have the needle pointing true north and south. That It was computed would occur somewhere about the year 2000., After that the deviation would be easterly. Lord Kelvin had shown from that tendency of the needle that the magnet- ism of the earth was slowly rotating round the Pole, that was, the magnetic pole of the earth was slowly performing a revolution round the North Pole. It was calculated that it would take something like 900 years ta revolve once round the Pole. It seemed very probable that something of the sort was going on in the earth's magnetism and producing those peculiar variations of the needle from year to year. The position of the mag- netic needle he pointed out was the position of the greatest cold. The greatest cold lay somewhere about extremities of the earth's magnet and not of the axis of the earth. There was therefore a curious connec- tion between the magnetism of the earth and the temperature of the earth. The deviation of the needle was to the east of its normal position in the cold weather and in the summer weather to the west. 'There they had a remarkable cennectioa between the pesitien of the magnetic needle and the temperature of the atmosphere. A reason for that had been conkctured by Professor Balfour Stuart, but had not been verified. During the day the needle moved in a very systematic way. It moved considerably towards the east in the coldest part of the night, and as the atmosphere became warmer it went towards the west and reached an extreme point at the hottest part of the day—about two o'clock in the afternoon. As the day begin to cool it went back to its normal position. When the needle at Kew was most to wards the east, at Hobart Town in Van Diernan's Land it was most towards the west, and vice 'versa. The motions were just reversed on each siie of the world, and that corresponded very much with the curious reversal of temperature on the tw sides of the world. They would expect that just. near the equator there would be very little motion. Observatioes ehewed that that was the case. In a place called Trevandrurn,whieh lay near to the equator. observations; had been takem and it was found that there was only the very slightest irregularity. That made them look for come cause which was common to them both. That cause undoub- tedly was the action of the sun. Observations of the sun's surface showed that the sun broke out into a mass of spots more frequently soma times teen at others, and it was found that once every 10 years it was very freely spotted. In the period between it was almost clear. In 1893 there was a great number of spots, the very largest spot ever seen being ebserved that year. Five years from now they might expect to have a recurrence of the outbreak. Observations had been made for 100 years and the period of ten years was very definitely fixed. The aurora borealis was probably due to electrical turbences of the atmosphere, and sometimes they took the form of streamsrs,which seemed to all tend to a cer- tain point. If they prolonged those streamers in imagin- aticn they would find that they united just over *toe top of file north magnetic pole. They therefore had a con- nection established between the aurora borealis and the magnetic pole of the earth. Beyond the reviler deviation of the maenetic needle there were at times certain irregular changes, sudden movements, eastwar or westward, and it had been made out that in certain years those irregular disturbances were more frequent than at other times, and that just; whea the sun spots were most frequent the magnetic needle was moss die- turbed. There was some very intimate connection be- tween the earth's magnetism and the action of the slur. They found that the auroras were more frequent when the sun spots blazed out most and the magnetic needle was most violently disturbed, and that established the eonnection between the three things. That ten years' period was very interesting. At that time it WiFt no- ticed that the rivers were fuller than at other timer and that there was a heavier rainfall. That had be traced very clearly. Supposing the sun was very active they would expect it to produce a good deal of fruit. Some- thing of the kind had actually been traced its Germany, where records had been kept of the grnpo crops for a long number of years, and it was found that once evert, ten years there was an exeeptionally good crop. In the way they would expect good harvests every ten years. They were unable to trace that very definitely, but there was little doubt that there were periodic times when the harvests were better. Good harvests put people in good spirits and hopeful so that they became speculative. That led to what was called good trade, and they there- fore had alternations of good and bad trade every ten years. He did not wish to push that speculation too far, but he would throw out the suggestion that there was some human interest in this subject, which at firet sipeat leaked rather dry and scient:fic. The sun wao the life and movement and spirit of everything tha went on upon the earth's surface. They were dependent upon it in every detail—for energy, heat and light. How it was communicated to them was somewhat of a mystery, but the probability was that its communication was similar to the communicetion in wireless telegraphy• They would see that enorrno is information had teen acquired about the earth's maenetism, and it was load- ing to some very interesting and extra ordinary ia.sults. (Applause.)

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

ST. PETER'S CHURCH.

At this church on Sunday Mr. T. E. Gibson was the special svaker at the weekly meeting of the Mea's Bible Class. Mr. Gibson, basing his address upon the words "For our citiznship is in Heaves," spoke of the duties, national and municipal, devolving upon Caristia-a men, and of the responsibility resting upon them that in the discharge of these duties they were governed aril con- trolled by Christian principles. Mr. E. H. Pearce, Nvis the soloist. He gave a true interpreta- tion of "Those who it in darkness," and he was equally successful in "Arm, arm, ye brave." So pleased were the members with both speaker and singer, that at their reqaeit Mr. Gibson promised to continue his remarks next Sunday afternoon, upon "The entrance into the Kingdern," and Mr. Pearce also kindly consented to give two more solos.

WYCLIFFE P.S.I. SOCIETY.

There was an average attendance at the meeting of this socir2ty on Slndav afternoon in Wycliffe Church. Mr. David Owen presided, and delivered a short address in which he drew some useful lessons from the life of Brown,of Manchester, delivered an addresS on the work of the Royal National Mission for Deer Sea Fishermen. He spoke of the great service the mission had been to the 20,000 men who lived on the wild North sea catching and sending fish to the English markets. Tho hard lot of these men was faithfully depicted, and the sly aker stated that it was not until the mission was started that the Brltish people began to take an interest in them. The society had now eleven boats on the North Sea and three off the coast of Labrador. Four of these wete fitted.up as floating hospitals, each containing a medical man who was kept constantly busy attending to the fi3herman. The mission was entirely unsectarian, and the services held were well attended. F-r the win- ter months, parcels of warn clothhaz were forwarded to the men. He appealed tor the sympathy and help of his hearers on behalf of thes:, fishermen.—Mr. W. H. Payton contributed a solo on the organ.—The service was also takrai part in by the Rev. John Yonge. Eleven new members were enrolled, making a total of nearly 600.

GOSPEL HALL, FORSTER-STREET.

On Sunday night Messrs. Dale and Winter of Man- chester preached here, basing their remarks on !Exodus 12 and Luke 4. 16-20, using the story of the Israelites bondage in Egypt to illustrate man-'s condition in the present age, in bondage to sin and Satan. The cruel tre.:.ment of the Egyptians caused them to cry to God for deliverance though there seemed to be no hope of escape, but God heard them and delivered them in hie own appointed way as told iu Exodus 12 by the blood of tke slain Lamb. This is one of God's pictures, thrown on the canvas of the Old Testament, and explained in the New Testament. Men to-day are in a far worse bondage than ever held the Israelites, but not till they resin their undone, lost, and ruined condition do they cry to God for mercy and deliverance,bur God in infinite love and compassion, gave His Son, the Lord Jesus Christ, who came into the world to save ui sinners. We deserved death and separation from God but Christ, the Lamb of God, undertook to be our substitute and to die in our place. Hs bore the punishment due to us and now a just God is able to offer pardon, forgiveness, deliverance, eternal life to ,all who will claim it, but to rej, ct this the only means of escape means remaining in bondage now and through all eternity. TETE liberation of Captain The P4rdon of DREYFUS has been an- Dreytus.

nounced, and he is a free man again. President LOUBET, in consultation with with his Cabinet on Tuesday, decided upon a pardon, but the official promulgation of it was withheld for a day or two in order to get DREYFUS quietly out of the country and avoid inconvenient demonstrations. The liberation of the man who has endured a martyrdom is matter for congratu- lation to himself, his friends, and all who have worked for justice. He is not acquitted of the crime laid to his charge ; the legal establishment of his innocence and restoration of his civil rights are objects yet to be sought. That the " pardon" is illogical goes without saying. It is equally illogical whether DREYFUS be innocent or guilty. If innocent, a " pardon " is an absurdity, although, one with which we are not unfamiliar ia this country. If Thugy-eus be really guilty oi the treason to his country imputed to him, the " pardon " is unwarrantable ; there is no justification for it. But the very fact that the French Government has resolved upon the pardon is in itself an acknowledgment that, despite the verdicts of two courts-martial, DREYFUS is held innocent of the crime of which he has been convicted, and that his conviction was against the Lurden of the evidence. It implies also that the suspicion, if not the belief, that DREYFUS has been made a scapegoat for the sins of others,whom,however,it is not convenient at this juncture to bring to justice. It is understood that with the pardon of DREYFUS other prosecu- tions, or projected prosecutions, are to be dropped. This will mean that the venal members, from MERCER downwards, for the time being escape their deserts. But the "pardon " must be regarded as only one step towards the even- handed justice that awaits all concerned in the conspiracy against the innocent. DREYFUS and his friends will start work for the restitu- tion of his rights, and the full recognition of his innocence. The French Government will probably continue its investigations, and at some future time we shall see a further denaansent,when the justice meted out will he rnora stern and logical. By the pardon of DREYFUS France is saved the ignominy of an innocent man's dying in gaol. The victim's health has suffered under the accumulated tortures heaped upon him. A free man once more,restored to wife and famiIy,ALFEED DREYFUS may regain his health and strength and live to see the discomfiture of his enemies. Buls at present all that is uncertain. One brave man who had struggled hard for justice, M. SCHEURER.* ICEsTNER,.the ex-Senator who first became con- vinced that the court-martial of 1894 had perpe- trated a judicial crime, has. passed away just as his labours were bearing fruit. Reputations have been both made and lost over the DREYFUS case, and there are reputations still lying tinder the cloud it has cast, some of which may be cleared, but others may be blasted. The pardon of DREYFUS affords a welcome respite from the heat and stress of the long struggle; it will inspire hope for the futurei but real and substantial justice has yet to be done.

CONTINENTAL journals have British and Russian • trade in the Esst. given us more than one hint that, in the event of war in South Africa, both Russia and Germany would take advantage of Great Britain's preoccupation to mak further encroachments in the East. That • is very probable, and France likewise might at- tempt further expansion had she not just now her own hands tied by domestic troubles. So far as Russia is concerned her ambition is insatiable and her activity tireless. Prince 00CtiTOMSNI'5 organ, the St. Petersburg " Viedomosti," is con- tinually urging an aggressive policy all along the line in Southern Asia. At the present time such policy is actually being carried out in Persia and Afghanistan, but it is ostensibly confined to CO merce. One of the semi-official Russian journals has lately boasted that, although the Amber of Afghanistan is precluded by treaty with Great Britain from diplomatic relations with Russia, since the Russian Trans-Caspian rail- way has approached the Afghan frontier the trade of Afghanistan with India has fallen off and been diverted into Russian hands. The statistics it gave on this head, if correct, certainly went far to bear out its contention that the trade and influence of Russia in Afghanistan were rapidlY growing again. Now a correspondent of the "Times "has been writing to shew that Russia is monopolising' the trade of Persia, more particu- larly in Khorassan. While the British Govern 4 ment thought it unnecessary "to put a single Consular agent between Meshed and Teheran on the west,and between Meshed and Persian Gulf 011

the south, the Russians had established them all over the province," where' they vi„,roroualy push Russian commercial interests. Moreover, Russia has compelled the Parsian Government to construct, at great expense, a good road from Meshed, the chief town of Khorassan, across the frontier, where it connects with the Trans-Caspian railway. As a result of these meas- ures "the whole province is already completely un- der Russian influence, and the British trade, once supreme there, has received its death-blow." Two articles only of Indian produce still hold their own—tea and indigo; but that is because Russia cannot produce them. They have to be brought by sea to Bandar-Abbas, and thence to be carried along a neglected road.eight hundred and seventy miles to Meshed, and they Feld o rn take less than three or four months on the journey. Along the Whole line there is mot a single British official to see fair play for British trachrs. We have a Political Agent at Meshed, but he disdains to look after British trade interests. Formerly, too,British goods crossed the Persian and Afghan frontier into Irranscaspia and Bokhara, but since 1895 Russia haserected an almost impassable wall of tariffs and prohibitions. Rather late in the day, the Govern- ment of India is trying to open a new trade route from Quetta to Meshed,a distance of one thousand miles. The Russian G wernmentrhas countered it by placing a Vice-Consul at Sistan, whore it has "absolutely no trade nor political busin-ss what- ever." We have Consuls neither at Sistan nor at Bandar-Abbas on the Persian Gulf. Now that our Government has begun to appoint commercial agents abroad to watch British trade interests Persia ought not to be neglected. The writer of the article on "The White Man's Burden in China," in the Contemporary Review points out that prac- ally we have no "Intelligence Department" to obtain information for us:of what is passing in the Far East. We can do without an "Intelligence Department" moulded after the French model, but there is certainly a need for the appointment of more wideawake and intelligent agents.

Even in the land of the canny Scot, lunacy is increasing, though not at a rate which may be con- sidered at all alarming. The annual returns of the Commissioners of Lunacy for Scotland show that there is, as in this country, a steady growth in the number of insane people. One would have thought, carelessly may be, that the Scotsman with his caution and canniness would be the least likely person to lose hia mental balance, but figures show that he is no more exempt from such troubles than other people. Another point of view occurs to us,which is possibly the more correct one, though we do not vouch for it. Tne typical Scots- man is proverbially keener in the pursuit of " bawbees " than any other mortal, and the worry thus entailed upon him, it seem only natural, should counterbalance the saving effect of his caution, and make him as liable to lunacy as every other competitor in the race of life. Thcse are but suppositions, however, and to continue them further would, we fear, lead to a suspicion that our own "soft grey matter" was in a disor- dered state. Reverting to the statistics we find that the number of patients during the last year for which complete figures are available was 12,482 and the erpenditure on their maintenance and medical supervision amounted to £298,878. The totals are considerably more than double those of 40 years ago; but this is not a startling fact con- sidering the better registration since the ;sassing of, the Lunacy Act of 1857. The worse disclosure GI the figures is that relating to 1898 alone, dur- ing which year there was an augmentation of 487 eases—just about double the average increase of the five years 1891-95. This sudden advance is probably quite exceptional, and does not indicate anything which need cause alarm. Lunacy is certain to continue increasing while the feverish haste and hurry of the present age prevails, and the only consolation lies in the fact that the higher medical skill obtainable 'lessens the severity of the eases and reduces the mortality.

The character sketch of Mr. Chamberlain which appears in the "Young Man "will be read with sympathetic interest by friends and opponents alike. It depicts a man broad-based upon self- reliance. In politics enemies must be made, but there is this compensation, that the strenuous opposition of political foes does not extend to private life, and the man whom you feel it a duty to denounce in public may be your charming com- panion in private. Mr. Chamberlain's genius for acidulous retort has ever been a stumbling block in the way of those who might otherwise be dis- posed to do more justice to a,realiy striking career. Moreover, the Colonial Secretary does not own to any sentiment, and sentiment is a stronger link in the chain that binds humanity together than he or any other man of intense practi- cality has ever been induced to imagine. Thus in many respects he repels rather than attracts; but yet by sheer character he is attractive. ,No public personage of our epoch has so thorouehlv and convincingly illustrated Emerson's profound saying that with consistency a great man has nothing to do. It" is the hobgoblin of little minds adored by lit.tle s.tatesruen and philosophers." Future historians will perhaps point an accusing finger at the unbridled desire for mastery which has distinguished the Colonial Secretary's social, municipal, and political career. It is a fault, or not a fault--according to the temperament of the people who argue the question. But those " penal ' blindnesses upon such as have the unbridled desires' spoken of by St. Augustine in his memorable "Con- fessions" have fallen also upon Mr. Chamberlain, even as they fell upon Cardinal Manning, and it is only fair to remember this when as orators we are 'tempted to denounce, with undue warmth, those political or diplomatic doings which we do not concur in.

• 64 Mr. W. M. Ackworth's letter in the "Times' from Denver, Colorado, on American express speeds, directs attention to a matter which is of great concern to the business men of this country. fib points out that between Philadelphia and Atlantic City express trains cover a distance of fifty-five miles in fifty minutes, and that they do it safely and with comfort. There is one service between London and Brighton—fifty-two miles in an hour on Sundays, and a few minutes more on 4 week days—of which we may be proud, especially as it is accomplished with the disadvantage of several long tunnels and one sharp curve. There is. truth •in Mr. Ackworth's observation that we do not like to feel that the trains are going at a very rapid rate, and any improvement which is made in oar service must be in quickening the pace during the slower periods of the journey, and not in raising the maximum. The trouble is is getting into and out of the Metropolis. Everyone knows what a Waste of time there frequently is in covering the last few miles on the South Eastern to Charing- Cross, on the London and Chatham to Ludgate • Hill, and on the South Weston to Waterloo. The G-reatEastern Railway Company has been the most enterprising of the compaaies to recognise this difficulty by spending huge sums in the en- largement of Liverpool-street and the improvc - ment of the line near London. The South Wes- ': tern are following in their wake and we may hope for an improvement there. There must be no ad- ditional risk, but there should be a clearer way for express trains, and that can be accomplished only by more lines and platforms.

The members of the Warrington Rural District Council were gracious enough at their meeting last week to allow the press to report their de- liberations respecting the difficulty which has arisen with their workmcn regarding their hours Of labour. The Chairman seemed to have some doubts as to the advisability of doing so, and took the opinion of the members on the matter. To their credit, be it said, they decided to act in thoopen. The point at issue is a very simple one and there is not the slightest need for such a

matter to be discussed with closed doors. The road-men ask for a modification of the new regu- lations which compel them to work until four o'clock on Saturd /637 afternoon, and do not make any allowance for walking time when the work is over two miles distant. The request seems a reasonable one, and as it cannot be said that the Council treat their men with a lavish scale of pay, there should be no difficulty in arriving at a conclusion satisfactory to all concerned.

The patriotism of some of the Volunteer Corps up and down the country in offering themselves for service in South Africa is to be admired, but their wisdom is somewhat questionable. Accord- ing to the report of the School of Musketry for last year, more than half of the entire volunteer force are only second-class shots, and their defi- ciency in this respect will most certainly debar them from seeing active service at the C apo. It is all very well for the units of a fighting force to be eager and enthusiastic for the work before thern,but these qualities will stand them in poor stead against skilful and courageous foes unless they are hacked up by military efficiency. Our friends, the Boers—with whom we hope we may not come in conflict—would soon make short work of a volun- teer corps by sheer superiority in marksmanship. The previous Boer war was a decidedly bloody one, due as much to the deadly shooting of the Dutch settlers as to anything else, and if there is to be another it will behove our Government to send out only picked troops. Our volunteers will do very well at home—where their proper place is— and if they want their patriotism taken advantage of, let them wipe out first of all, the reproach with regard to the handling of their weapons. To de- velop into first-class shots they will have to work hard, especially considering the way they are handicapped by the scarcity of suitable riff ranges.

It seems just withia the possibilities of science that ere long the blind may be enabled to see. A Russian electrician, Dr. Peter Steins, has corns forward with an idea which promises much, and performs not a little. Dr. Steins says he has an electrical apparatus which will enable blind men to see with their brains. He has been interviewed, and by the aid of his apparatus, which is presum- ably after the style of the Riintgenray instrument, has succeeied in making his interviewer, while thickly blindfolded, clearly discern a pair of pincers held before him. Now this is an accom- plishment of no small importance in itself, and encourages one to hope that he may fulfil his promise to enable blind people to see with their brains Pot as ordinary folk do with their eyes. Dr. Steins' apparatus really performs the functions of the eyes in that it conveys an impres- sion of things external to the brain. It is just a question whether people whose optic nerve is use- less will be benefited to the extent imagined. The interviewer was not deficient in this respect, and we must await actual experiments on the blind be- fore we can place faith in the savant's words. There seems to be some hope for the blind how- ever. If the X-rays can show us a bullet embedded in the human frame there seems no reason why some kindred application of science should net carry an image to the brains of the blind.

RUNCORN NULES.

[By A. CORREsPONDENTA The strides made by the Runcorn Co-operative Society are again illtptrated by the tact that the society a few weeks ago completed the negotiations for the purchase of the premises lately occupied by Mr. W. Collier, grocer, in Church-street. Tb.o free- hold of this property, which is computed to be worth £4,000, was secured by the Society for some £2,250.

A severe storm which swept over the district in the early part of the week had the effect of bringing down numbers of loose slates ia and about Duke's fiel?.3 to the great danger of p%ssers-by. Luckily, however, no serious accideats resulted. One often wonders how so many slates happen to be loose, awaiting each succeeding high wind. But wonder as one will the slates are there, and when it blows, down they come, and often enough some one is in- jured. If property owners were a little more care- ful of their property, and would do something to these loose slates before they became the deadly missiles they often ara, a public service would be achieved.

Now the winter nights are coming on the ques- tion of amusements, and of how to spend the long lonesome evenings is being widely discussed by the average Runcornian. This brings to our notice the Theatre Royal, the Public Hall, the Club, the Public Library, and the various concerts &c., that aro promoted from time to time. Bat when the whole are summed up, the truth must be admitted that the average Runcornian has precious little amuse- ment provided him wherewith to kill his time, and when the home accommodation is so scant, it is no small wonder that he goes afield ad spends the evening in the "'city."

An interesting question awaiting solution is that concerning the Manchester Ship Canal, in respect to its true nature as affected by the River's Pollution Act? Is the Canal a stream, a river, a canal, or as some people seem to think, a sewer? Those at present seem to be the four dfiaitione assigned to it. But, whatever view is taken,it mast be admitted that the position of the canal is some- where " betwixt tha devil and the deep sea for, on the one hand, it is contended that it waters are abominably contaminated with sewage, and that the effluvia arising therefrom in its higher reaches is most obnoxious ;yet the canal cannot prevent the admission of this sewage. On the other hand, the Upper Mersey has been adjudged a stream under the meaning of the Act ; and, any matter escaping from the canal into the Mersey—and it is only natnral to suppose SOMa does so escape—will undoubtedly be treated by the Navigators as pollution. Thus matters are supposed to stand at present, and until the exact state of affairs is defined the subject is one likely to often engele the attention of the Runcorn Rural District Council.

So far as I can ascertain the strike amongst sailors and firemen has not cast its baleful influence upon the port of Runcorn, e,nd traffic anrongst b 3th ocean going and coasting craft of all kinds seems to be progressing as briskly as ever. Other trade indications also point to a prosperous winter all round and on the authority of a large employer of labour I have it that there need not be a single nyin out of work in Runcorn this winter. There is plenty to do all round. This op;Amisti3 iwof things is very gratifying.

The enthusiasm of the Runcorn Antlers' Asiocia- tion has led them to build a refreshment. tent near to their waters at the Old Quay,which it is intended to fit no with cooking apparatus, so that the' very 'ardent " can with the greate‘t comfort spend es much time as possible on the spo'.;. The tent, which is to be of a substantial character, is being erected by the members themselves and does them great credit. It will also serve as a store for the mem- bers' tackle.

TUE LINNETS WIN. BUT HOW?

BY THE "SCORCHER."

Runcorn won again, and on Saturday com- pleted Vie " hat-trick " as I expected. I was not, however, greatly satisfied with the game; which did not come within "streets "of the exhibitions against Swinton and Broughton Rangers. But mien, Runcorn, if they are anything, are chival- rous; and as Wines only came with half-a-team they generously let them off with balf-a-wacking. Possibly—mind I don't assort it—but possibly it was so. Football, you know, is full of surprises,and Saturday's game, between the two locals, was no ' e -ception. -.Mach of the "raggedness' shown could traced to a preponderance of local feeling. For, as is readily admitted, a too exuberant display of that explosive ever made to sp ail things. Ipse fact,; one cannot have the man and the ball, but mast sacrifice one for the other. Thus there was a rot visible. and as one looked at the guns and found cause enough to shoat himself blue in the face, Fein SD nettling was" raggy "—nothing went smoothly. ern had the same men, wao rendered each good service against the James's; but the dashing I finiehed display that brought proud Broughton to I their feet was absent. The scrummages were such as I wish not to behold again; rough and tumble seemed to be the order in a" whirligig of dis- order." Everyone played well at that. Occasion- ally, however, the Runcorn backs did put in a little good; but so few and far betweaa were these dis- plays, they could be likened to the fitful lightning of a waning storm. Widnes played anyhow. Ran- corn tried their best to stop thent,—which, by the way, they did—then they went and played any- how themselves. Why they did me, goodness enly knows? Had they, on the other hand, stopred Widses' little game, and played football themselves the result would have been far 1310°S gratifying. Some peaple have expressed wonderment at how Runcorn beat Swinton and Broughton, yet, had they seen those two matches they would simply wonder whatever was up with them that they let Widnes get as danger- ous as they sometimes did. One thing is,hewever, certain, such form was neither cup form nor championship form, and we must have no more of it. But to return to facts: Runcorn won, and having won must be excused. For as "charity covers a multitude of sins," " two points "cover an afternoon's wretched football, To beat Oldham to-morrow, Runcorn will have to play just a trifle better than anything they have yet done, and keep it up, too, all the way. They must remember to go the" whole hog." No half measures. Let them beat Oldham in the pack and the game is theirs; but,once let the Oldham backs getit into their heads that they are behind the strongest pack, and Run- corn will get such 'a drubbing as will make them bu. . y Vaeir heads in shame.

On Sunday afternoo,l a somewhat alarming collision occured on the Great Central Railway Campany's line between St. Helens and Havdock. The line has not yet been opened for passenger traffic, but it is rapidly approaching completion, and it is exp-cted that pass- enger trains will be running in the cJurse of the sinxt few weeks. It appears that yesterday afternoon two of the goods engines, through some unexplained cause, were travelling on the Same line of rails, and came into collision. ALLEGED EMBEZZLEMENT BY A COMMERCIAL TRAVELLER.

At the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday, Frederick W. Poole. a respectably dressed man, was charged on a warrant with embezzling the sum of £3, the moneys of Messrs. J Crosfield and Sons Limited, on the 10th November 1898. and later within a period of six months, the sum of £8 Bs. 64.—Mr. Browne appeared for the prosecutora, and Mr. Thomas S. Steel defended, —Mr. Browne said that the prisoner had not only embezzled the sums named in the charge, but another £2O which he kept with him for current ex- penses. He was a traveller for the firm and his salary was £3 a week and all expenses. The district he worked was around Norwich and Ipswich, and his duty WAS to receive the money from customers and either send it to Warrington or bank Mat the place where he was staying. Everything went on all right for atime, but on the 14th November Messrs. Crosfield received a certain state- ment (handed to the magistrates) and later on had a letter stating that t3risoner had lost the money and promised to make it good, but he suddenly disappeared from Ipswich. It was found out that his furniture was stored in London,and so the police watched for him,and he was afterwards arrested. He intended to plead net guilty. It would be necessary to hring three witnesses, and,the sum embezzled being aver 40s..prisoner should be committed to the Quarter Sessiens for trial. A repre- sentative from Messrs.Crosfield's wcrks would swear that the letter, Ecc., was written by the prisoner.— Mr. Robert Bruce Murray, an accountant in the employ of Messrs Crosfield said that the money had never been paid in. It was prisoner's duty to send the money to Warrington or ply it into the bank. The total amount musing WAS between £3O and £36, £2O of which he had been allowed for his excenses.—Mr Steel said that prisoner's wages had been reduced to £2 which ameunt included his expenses. Witness said he did not know of that.—Mr Steel said that Poole had in;tructed him to say that whatever money he had rec, ived had been paid in to the credit of Messrs Crosfield; and if it had not arrived at Messrs Cresfield's it was not his fault.—Mr Browne asked where he had paid the money in.—Mr Steel said he 1-,ad not had the opportunity of ascertaining that as prisoner was only brought to Warring,ton the previous night. He understood that Mr Browne would ask for a remand for a week. He should like the magistrates to

make an order for to prisoner remain in the cells at War- rington so that he might be able to see him and obtain more instructionfrom him.—Prisoner was remanded for a w.ek. the Bench granting the order for him to remain at Warrington.

The members of this choir had a most enjoyable trip to Llandudno on Monday. The party lett Bank Quay Station at 8-17 am., in a special saloon, and arrived at their destinatien in good time. The catering was entrusted to the managers of the Nietropole Hotel, where dinner and tea were served in first class style to the satisfactioa of all the visi- tors. After dinner the party drove to Conway Castle and greatly enjoyed an inspection of the ruins of the hietorical building. The weather was very fine and the drive was greatly appreciated. After tea, the presentation of a handsome toilet set as a wedding gift to Mrs. Thompson, a late member of the c'loir, was made by Mrs. Taylor, who, in a few well-chosen w3rls, wished the bride and her husband much happiness. Mrs. Thompson very feelingly responded, and thanked the members of the choir for their kindness and good wishes. Votes of thanks were proposed by Mr. Peter Harrison(the organist) t 3 Mr. Taylor and the trustees of Bold- street for generously defraying the cost of the picnic, also to Mr. Cooks, station master at Bank Qoav, for providing the saloon.

EARLESTOWN V. SOUTHPORT CENTRAL. LANCASHIRE LEAGUE.—Earlestown's third fixture in this league was decided on Saturday, when they met Southport Central, at Southport. The Central are considered extremely dangerous opponents on their own ground ,and it was no matter of surprise when it became known in Earlestown that victory rested with Southport by five goals to nil. The game was played before about 2,500 spectators in fine weather. The Central started and faced the sun. The opening exchanges were very even, but the Central soon dashed off, and the Earlestown backs were kept busy. fleyes cleared his lines, however, and midfield play followed. From a free kick Wilmington had a grand chance of scoring. He missed the opportunity. but Baxendale nipped in and scored a soft goal fifteen minutes after play commenced. Baxendale had another opening immediately afterwards, but delayed shooting, and was smartly robbed. Cunliffe sent wide, but Hal- lows nearly scored with a clever screw shot which struck the net. Cunliffe, however, put the rCentral two in front with a fine shot, which Chew missed altogether. Settle and Lincoln were conspicuous for Earlestown, hut the attack was smartly repulsed. Lang scored a third god. Half-time—Southport, 3; Earlestown, 0. Final— Southport Central, 5 ; Earlestown, 0.

GOLDEN WEDDING CELEBRATION AT

WARRINGTON.

It is not often that a married couple live to cele- brate their golden wedding, but when such a happy circumstance does occur it is only natural that steps should be taken to fittingly celebrate the event. Such was the case this week when Mr. and Mrs. Job Swales, formerly of Warrington, but now of Lowton, completed 50 years of married life. There was a happy gathering of relatives and friends in the Primitive Methodist Schoolroom, Legh-street, on Tuesday last week, when the auspicious occasion was suitably recognised. The company numbered about eighty, and included about 40 children and grandchildren, and a like number of relatives and friends Mr. and Mrs. Swales, who looked extremely well, and upon whom 50 years of married life seemed to have pressed lightly, were the recipients of numerous presents. Fifty years ago last Sunday they were married in St. Paul's Church, Bewsey-road, and for the greater por- tion of the period since then, Mr. Swales has been in business in the town, at one time conduct- ing a brickmaking bueinese. Their seven married children were all present on Tuesday, namely: Mr and Mrs Robert Swales, (Manchester) Mr and Mrs J II Swales (Manchester) ; Mr and Mrs John Podmore (Warrington) ; Mr and Mrs M Gregory (London) ; Mr and Mrs J Broadhurst', War- rington); Mr and Mrs George Titchett (Warrington), and Mr and Mrs W H Covell (Manchester). The friends and relatives present included Mr and Mrs John Henry Knowles; Mr and Mrs Joseph Knowles; Mr and Mrs Smith Knowles, (Sale) ; Mr and Mrs Joseph Podmore ; Rev. C Finch, Rev. M Ii Chapman (Widnes) ; Mr Crampton, Mr Crampton, jun., Mr Joseph Titchett, Mr Gleave, Mr Spruce (Preston Brook),Sir Stephen Dale,Mr Joseph Bissell Mr S Rowlance and others. An excellent tea was provided,the catering being in the hands of Mesdames Cooke, Cotton, John Pod- more and Broadhurst. At the conclusion, Mr John Podmore, the eldest sou-in-law, in a few brief but appropriate sentences proposed the toast of " The health of Mr and Mrs Swales." Mr. SMITH KNOWLES in seconding the toast, said such an occasion did net often happen; in fact, it was somewhat rare for a man and woman to live together in married life—and happy married life— for 50 years. (Hear, hear.) The speaker proceeded to refer in a jocular manner to his early recollec- tions of Mr. Swales' courtship when he took from him his sister and companion. Mrs. Swales at this moment still looked healthy and happy, and Mr. Swales resembled a patriarch of Israel, and was looking better than for many years past. (Hear, hear.) He had been Possessed of a pretty equable temperament, and had got through life fairly com- fortably. They were all sincerely grateful to have that opportunity of tendering their congratu- lations to Mr. and Mrs. Swales, and trusted that both of them might be spared to enjoy each others company for many years to come. (Applause.) Mr. J. W. KreowLEre also spoke in support of the toast, and congratulated Mr and Mr.s Swales on the celebratioa of their golden wedding. He gave some pleasing lecollectione and concluded by ex- pressing the hope that God might spare Mr and Mrs Swales for many years mere. In responding to the toast, Mr. SWALES paid a tribute to the services of the help-meet he bad bad during his life. They had been very happy and comfortable, and were all the better for the troubles, trials and difficulties they had experienced. Ile was conscious of God's sparing mercies, and his religion Was a source of consolation to him in the evening of his days. He was very pleased to see his rela- tives and friende,aud desired to thank them for their presence and hearty congratulations. ( Applause.) Mr. GREGORY expressed the wish of the. company that Mrs Swales should say a few words Mrs. S WALES expressed the pleasure it gave her to meet them all ; au e hoped that if they did not meet together again in this world, they would do so in the heavenly kingdom. The subsequent proceedings were of a social character. Congratulatory addressee were given by Messrs. Stephen Dale, Joseph Titchett, Joseph Bis- sell and M. Gregory. The addresses were agreeably interepersed with music supplied by Mr. Frank Podmore, Master and Miss Gregory. Miss Elsie Clrereory, Mr S liowlance and Mr spruce. Refresh- ments were served. and the gath-riog diepersed about nine o'elock.

THE cossEßvATives AND TETE MAYORALTY.

The ConsFirva"iv- members of the Town Council have decidPd to mac Mr. J. Carlton Parr to accept the Mayoralty br the entiuinf veer, becau le they think "the time his arrive] when hi a (Mr. PArr';) eminent movie's to the town should be recognised " and because some- thing shaula he don's to rea‘tora the " dienity " of tOe Corporation and they think Mr. Parr is the proper man to do it.

Noce, if the 'reties whe attended the meeting at which this" moiniAa ous " decieion was arrived at had been wise they woolci have remembered that the Council is compreied ef 36 members,a large minority of whom are Libra's, and whom it weuld have been advisable and only ceurtelus to consult. Mr. Parr has the reputatien of being a gentleman and it will surprire one if be accepts the Ma.eo•alty offeteJ to him in so impelite a manner. It is ee:y refreshing to keiow that the Tory members of the C,nncil think it is time something was done to re- stere trip " dignity "of the Corperatien. But who pray is respousible for its eiaet ef dienitc? Tha scene enacted at the last Council Meeting shows c'early that the Tory party alone is responsible. For years past they have done their tylit tie exclude Liberals of position from every office, they have kept the Mayoralty exclusively in their own hands for nearly 30 years (with tile exception of one year) and now that they have used up all the material, which they consider suitable for the Mayoralty, very suddenly diecover that this is a " fitting" time to recos.,nele Mr. Parr's eminent services to the town. What a piece of transparent nonsense! Everybody knows perf-ctly well what this little dodge means. If the Torte had really wished to recognise Mr. Parr's eminent service to the town, why have they been so long in giving effect to their desir-s ? The fact is that rather than do th• fair and honourable thing and give the Liberals a look in at the office, they will descend to any subterfuge. It is pleasing to know, however, that the Tory meeting was not particularly well attended and that there are several members of their party who are not prepared to be coerced into a line of action which is a stuc.iad insult to their Liberal colleagues and utterly destroys any compliment which might otherwise be peid to Mr. Parr. Ti/GH TIDE IN THE MEasEY. Thera was a very high tide at Warriug, on at 1.30 on Wednesday afternoon, the footpath under Arpley Railway Bridge being several feet under water. The fields are Arpley were flooded, but for- tunately no cattle have been lost. There was a very high flood tide in the river Mersey on Tuesday night, at Fiddler's Ferry. In some places the water overflowed the banks, the low-lying fields being inundated, the Marsh being several feet under water. Mr. John Humphries. the Marsh keeper,was engsged throughoqt the night in getting the cattle off the Manh. LAMM BKST GLACE BOOTS AND SHORS UNDER THE CLOCK, RHIDGH-STHRET.—Advt. RINGTON I ISTIIICT.

The quarterly meeting of the " Late Spring "lose" Lodge took place on Saturday eveniugat the Barley Mow Inn, Market-street. W.M. James Clare presidad, being supported in the vice-chair by D.M. Joseph Booth. The reports were very satisfactory showing a gain of £46 for the quarter. The total number of financial members is 380 with a capital of £1,684. After the ordinary business the President callei Upon P.M. John Jones to make a presentation to P.M. William Sollory, of a handsome framed emblem of merit for services rendered to the Lodge. Bro. Jones in making the presentation on behalf of the lodge took occasion to remark that societies such as tte one of which they were members were often very much indebted to active and useful members who were alwaye ready to give their services for the good of the society without any expeetation of pecuniary recompense. Ihe embleixi he was about to present was in grateful acknowledgement of such ser- vices and as a token of respect and esteem of the lodge toward Bro Sollory. The emblem was then presented to him with hopes that he might keg continue in a career of usefulness to the lodge. Bro Sollory returned thanks for ti.e very handsome girt he had recAved and for the very flattering re- marks from Bros Jones, Parr, Gleave, and Whitlow. ,

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. EBY "OvausT."] I feel bound, already, to give the Warrington teas i a warning. They are doing past what they did last Ykar, following out the results one by one, and Pc ipt an average of a point per match. This is not whit is expected, of them. Another season like the last v, ill go war to killing Rugby football in Warrington...lSO it behoves the team to strike a new line entirely. The first three matches of last September resulted in a draw, a loss, and a win, and this September the opening trio of competition contests have done just the Sarzejhing. Of course, the result might be much worse, but,l am really concerned lest tin Wirepullers should slip ll:Act:the rut wherein they wallowed last season. Match N0.'44 of the opening month was at Widnes, and there another defeat was experienced. This. time it is again on foreign soil, but the valley of the Roach shoold br more productive of points than the battleground of the " Chemicals. It is in this match, the concluding one of the present month, that I expect the Warringtonians to climb out of the rut, and afterwards keep out. If they fail at Rochdale the coincidence will be so strong that lam not sure whether I shall have the slightest hope left of last season's, die- astrous experiences being improved upon. But then the Committee and players alike say the Hornets are going to be squelched, and, apparently, they mean it.

Stockport were made short work of on Saturday and this encourages the view taken of the tussle with, the Rochdale men. Just to show a little pride I may say that it is not every team that can threw off five picked backs and win a match with their successors. • Yet this is what Warrington did. Bate and Ashurst were the sole survivors of the back division which did duty—sr attempted t3—agaiest Leigh. AU the threequarters,ao the full back, were replaced by others and th* result was much more satisfactory than the mcet sanguida,Sup- porter could have etpected. I must not omit to mea- tion that Fish and Harris were absent through injury, but the fact remains that the three- quarter line as constituted on Saturday aequittsd itself really well. Three of the four men were responsible for 15 points between them, and as no one else scored they may be said to have done theirlshare towards securing the victory.

The Cheshire men came to Warrington with the rosiest mectations. There is no doubt about that. Had they not vanquished the once formilable Wiganers by la points the previous week? Ceitsinly they had, and they looked upon the conquest of Warringten as well-nigh a certainty. This was a mistake, for there are no certainties-1 ispeot- ball? It might have turned out all right from their point of view if Warrington had not bten so desprrately eager to atone for the loss of two points the previoul, weir. They neglected to consider tbe fond aspirationsif th,gr visitors and gave them a drubbing of a truly unmerci- ful character—which was ungiad. But then kindness, like the certainties, is quite unknown in football. All a team has to think about is the most effectual method oi knocking Gut another team in a given space of timo. Warrington happened to have tilt best thinking and knocking out apparatus on Saturday. That was all.

Notwithstanding the previous week's dissppointment the spectators rolled up in large numbers. They had not seen Warrington's new mea yet, and they meant to de so or know why. Now that they have seen them they will probably want to see more,ani the club treasurer will not be the man to stop them. He has only one motto and that is " bet 'em all come." And for the last two weeks they have come in a fashion which betokens inuels good. Ther. were many new faces in the Stockport ranks—importa- tion has been wholesale in that part of Cheshire—but one was as familiar as that of the town clock. Fred Saville is a popular visitor:lto the Wild lespoll arena, and his abilities as a footballer have always commanded respect; they do so still, thnueh not quite to the same extent perhaps. Saville's burly figure was almost as prominent on Saturday as of yore, and once it very nearly found its way across the Warriegton lin.. Tnera were one or two small impediments in the way, however. Of the strangers not mush can be said. They did nit appear to be equal to the old brigade, though one or tw, of them payed in a style of some promise. Al an orgaeization, holsever, they were of little account, and Warrington will not have many such easy journeys to success in the contests to came. Saville was the best of the lot, and next to him came Trotter and Oram, forwards, and Gough, half-back.

The home lot seemed of entirely different ealibre, Jones especially earning distinction. For a man t) ob. tain 3 goals and a try on the occasion of his first appear- ance in his clubs arena is a recommendation ta popu- larity which requires some beating. The crowd already weirs to make an idol of J3nes, but be will make a big mistake if he allows them to do anything of the sort. A man who is idolised thinks more about the men who wsrship him than he doss aLont the game. Jones ap- pears to bs a player who plays the game as it should be played, however, and his display at centre on Saturday fully satisfied the critics. The Stockport officials seemed a bit sorry that he • was not on their Bide. whiah is net very surprising for they seem badly off for a capable man in this position. A feature ot the game was Joe( s's goal-kioking, which faisly opened all eyes. The last goal he lan-'ed, from touch, was equal to anything of the sort which has been put to the credit of Warrington for a long time. Isher- wood also suede a highly successful debut, and notwith- standing a sore knee he played one of his best games oa Saturday. C. Wood, the Llynwypia man, showed sur- prisingly good form compared with his displays of last season and he will prob•bly keep his place. Merrvweather on the other wing, was not brilliant, but his steadiness and resolute,tar.kling were of service snore than once. Moores was only fair at full back. He re- quires more practice with the "A" team yet. The only part of the home team which showed a pronounced weakness was behind the scrum. Bate and Ashurst are nothing like the pair they were, though the falling- off is principally on the part of Ashurst. Be seems quite oct of form and lost the team many chances by faulty passing. He will have to stand down for awhile. The for- wards, considering their lack of weight are parformiag very well, but a little of the north country blood, which is frequently talked of, is still required to make the eight really formidable.

The game itself on Satur •ay was an interesting one to watch, for the flashes of bright play were not infre- quent. Warrington went in for the open game I advo- vated and its success should encourage them to peraue it. They made it quite ev dmt that the ream possesses threequarters who can score if only the ball is passed out to them in a proper manner. Lt the:n get into a proper system of attack and they will do well. The de- fence is all right at any time There is still a inaric.d tendlncy to run the wing men into toach,and the centres must cultivate the art of naaking a way towards the goal rather than the corner-sag. Isherwood, for in- stsrice, might have much improved hi g fine run in the secmd half if he had only doubled inside Saville and made for the posts. As it was, he, Merryweather, and J ,nes were all bunched in the corner and the try was Dearly lost. I think with similar open tactics this Satur- day they should beat Rochdale Hornets. They have, moreover, the enc-airagement of two successes on the Hornets ground in the past two seasons, and the reputed bad form of their opponents. This latter must not be too much counted on however, for the IVehdalians are sure to be desperately anxious to notch their fir,t league point.

The League table up ti date, is as below. It affords an interesting study already with Runcorn on the top and Warrirwton 6h on the list. No less than four clubs are without a single point,while such cracks as Swinton and Broughton Rangers in a very humble position.

CYCLING.

I Oa Sunday morning, at six o'clock, J. W. Stocks corn- moncerl an attempt to make a motor-tricycle record be- tween Land's End ani John o' Groats. He fully ex- pected to accomplish the journey between the two , places in less than two days, which meant that his ' time between Land's End And Recoil% the half-way houso, would be under twenty-lour hours. He was thnod to be at Kendal at five o'clock on Monday morn- ing, but owinv. to breakdowns between Land's End and Exeter and the bal state of the reads he did not reach Kendal until nine o'clock on Monday morning. The conditions were all against him then,rain falling and the rw's loping heavy, awl he abandoned the attempt. He had then accomplished about four hundred miles. Up to Warrington, however, his time had been good, as he had done the 358 miles in nineteen and a quarter hours, or au average ot 182 miles per hour.

In cinnection with the ride it shluld 131 mentioned that the zealous police of Sleaford, Lincolnshire. nrese- cutel StockA for Niious riding, and a prwalty of £3 and coit I was imposed hv the insic.7istrares. Stocks did not app,ar or he might have got off with a more moderate fine.

SWIMMING GALA AT WARRINGTON. INTERNATIONAL WATER POLO MATCH.

ENGLAND V. WALES.

The War:ington Swimming Club has to be con- gratulated upon the success which attended the gala and International polo match at the Baths in Leh-street on Saturday evening. This is the first time we believe, tnat Warrington has been selected for an International match of any description, and it speaks well for the reputation which the War- rington bath has gained, that it should be chosen for SLICI an important event. It was also a further matter for congratulation that the Warrington Swimming Club has at least one member suffi.dently well qualified to receive international honours in Mr A. G. Robertson. On entering the bell on Saturday to take up his position in goal Rob.rtson received a flattering reception from his townspeople. In addition to the polo match there were several im- portant local eventc, including the schoolboy :;ham- pionship of Warrington and district and the chaoa- , pionship of ‘Varrington and district (150 yards). Dellite the enhanced prices which had to be , elarged for admission there was a large attendance. The local committee had made excellent arrange- im,,nts and everything passed off satisfactorily. The officials were the following ;—Judges, Messrs J Critchley, J Evans and T Locker ; starter, Mr J tterbyshire ; check starter, Mr G Grounds, and com- petitors' stewards, Messrs R I Harding and S J Beaman ; hon. secretaries, Messrs F Garside and A G Robertson ; hon. treasurer, Mr C H Wood.

50 YARDS HANDICAP.

The first event was a 50 yards handicap for members of the Warrington club only. This was decided in eight heats, the first in each heat being allowed to swim in the second round. The winners of heats were : J P Prince, A Baker, W Suttou, A Bosnow, J Graham, C Aslitou, W Win- stanley, and E A Jens. The competition generally was very keen, and ti-re was litt,la to CloOmt betwc,,n the swimmers. The second round left Boscow, Ashton, and Winstauley for the final, and they provided a very good race. Baker, who started scratch man and swam splendidly, won first honours, half a yard in front of Ashton, who touched a second before Boscce —F,rst prize, electro-plated tea and coffee service ; second, case of carvers ; third, gent's toilet a 99 ; fourth, gold film- albert.

THE POLO MATCH -ENGLAND WINS EASILY.

The event of the evening WAS the international polo match, and the interest of the epectatore may be sai to have been centred in it. It was perhaps, unfortenate that it should have proved such a one-sided affair for this detracted from the interest. Wales have only four clubs from whith to select an international polo team, and are therefore considerably handicapped in placing a team in the bath to compete with England with any chance of success. Their display on Saturday was some- what disappointing. There was a lack of combination, and their shots had no sting behind them. But this is anticipating the game somewhat. The teams entered the water as selected. They were composed as follows: England : A G Robertson (Warrington), goal ; A L Jones (Everton), and J E Taylor (Leicester). backs ; Sharrock (Wnite star), half back ; P C Robertson (London Polytechnic), J H Hellings (Ravensbourne), and P Ripley, captain (St. Helens). forwards. Wales : E C Lane (Newport), goal ; J Rowan (Swansea) and F C Lee (Penarth), backs ; Ii J Herbert (Newport), half back ; E A Fry, J Wallace, (Penarth) and W Doherty Swansea), ' forwards. The officials were : Referee, Mr J Carmichael (Ireland) ; timekeerer, Mr F R Edwards (hon. secretary N.C. A.S A.) ; and goal scorers : Messr, Rederiek Williams (prsident N.C.A.S.A.) and J J Neill (president of Welsh Swimming Association.) In the first half the Welsh team defended the deep end. Hellinge secured from the first throw out, and passed to Sharrock. A clever movement by that player enabled Robertson (the London Polytechnic) to get possession, but he was spoiled by a foul. A moment later the Lou. diner had a chance and sent in a shot which Lane smartly accounted for. The Welshmen, were, however, unable to clear their lines and Hellings took advantage of a clever pass, and scored the first goal. The defence of the English team was tested by Wallace, but Sharrock secured and sent in a beautiful long shot which Lane pur. into the net in attempting to clear. A brilliant passing movement by Jones and Ripley gave Sharrock a capital chance, but Fry fouled. Hellings, however took full advantage of the pass out, heating Lane with a hard shot. The Welsh players now exhibited a little spirit and played much more vigorously in theopen. Hollings was penalized and Doherty was able to give Wallace a chance, but the final effort went wide. Excel- lent play by the English team took the hall well into their opponents' half, and Lane was called upon to save two shots in rapid succession. Lee relieved and Wallace made a bid, but Taylor effected a beautiful save. k moment later 11)bertson WAS called upon, but the shot which was from Lee lacked sting and he had no difficulty in clearing. Following a spell of even play Ripley secured and after a fine dribble set in an excellent shot, which Lane was applauded for clear- ing. Shortly after Robertson had a couple of shots to deal with, hut he had no difficulty in keeping his goal intact. Sharrock took the ball well into the opposing half and passel to Robertson (London), who sc)red easily. Play continued round the Welsh goal and Lane had a hot time. He was repeatedly cheers d for brilliant saves, but could not entirely resist the onslaught, and Robertson added two more goals, the score at half- time reading England six coals Wales none. After the change of ends Robertson (London) was first conspicuous with a splendid shot, Lane having to concede a corner. There was no immediate result, but play continued dangerously near to the Welsh goal, and Sharrock bad hard lip's, the ball going over. Rowan took the ball away and gave to Herbert who neglected an excellent opportunity to score. A fine passing movement between Jones, Ripley. and Sharrock took the ball well up the bath, and Robertson (London) receiving in a favourable position added the seventh goal. It was a short-distance shot and Lane had no chance, Numerous fouls toek place, and for a minute or two the ball travelled quickly from end to end. Walface had a shot at goal, hut Robertson was found to be quite safe. Doherty and Wallace passed the English backs, and the former was fouled in front of goal. He was awarded a penalty. and Robert,ora was beaten by a hard shot. England reasserted themselves, and had the best of the play for the remainder of the match. Robertson (London) added another goal for them, and at the call of time the score was England eight goals, Wales one. '',4I7COIJNCILLOR PIERPOINT.AND WARRINGTON WATER.

Councillor PIERPOIIiT delivered a brief speech during a short intgival., He saii he thought the town of War- rington might congratulate itself that it possessed a bath ,of the character of the one before him. Many impor- tant races and contests had taken place in it, but he believed that was tbe first occasion upon which the bath had been chosen for such an important event as an international polo match. They might regard that as a sufficient credential that the bath was perfect in every respect. (Applause.) As a member of the Corporation of Warrineton, although he was proud of the bath, he was not proud of the water with which it was filled. (Wear, hear.) Though discoloured, how- ever, it was perfectly clean, and as the swimmers had suffered no inconvenience he would take no further exception to it. He supposed that owing to the long drought it bad had to be filled from Walton, and that would account for the darkness of the colour of the water. Besides having so good a bath there was another thing which Warrington might congratulate its-lf upon, and that was that it had so many very excellent swim- mers. (Applause.) That was a matter for great con- gratulation, and ke thought the Warringten Swimming Club might shake hands with itself upon the fleet that one of its members had been selected to play in so important an event as an international polo match. (Applause) He had had the pleasure many a time of seeing Rebert‘on between the sticks in the football ground, but he had never seen him keen goal at polo. He could ear, however, that if he was able to play poi Re well as he played f 4otball they would not have very much to complain about. (afar, hear.) A copy of tee second appeal issuee by the association was enclosed in the le;ter. an i was read by the secretary. It was as fellows :- To iac Trade Union- ist' of Great Britain arid Ireland.—WarringtoreSeptenn- ber 11th, 1899.—We,the filesmiths of Warrington, again earnestly appeal for support to enable us to continue our struggle with the naasters, who refuse to accede in any way to our demands fir a sliget increase its our wages, wilich are practically the same as they were 50 years ago. The trade unionists of this country will readily recegnise that we have serieus and just cause for complaint when we state that, after having served seven years' appreniieeship to the trade, our wagss on the whole do not exceed £1 per week. We cast refer with pleasure to the fict that we have been affiheted with the Warrington Trades and L abour Cauncil since its inception aticeit 25 years age. We are also ineorper- ateci with the Federated File francs of Great Britain. You will therefore see that we have embraced the great principle of ueity of action We can also refer t o the tact that since the year 1874 we have given to various trade disputes no less a sum than £5OO. which is a large amount considering that during the whole of that period we have nnt received anything in return. We have now been out 11 weeks, and there are no Si:NM indicative of settlement, the masters now (as from the first) ignoring our claims, and refusing to in any way bring about a , satisfactory settlement of tite dispute. rur brother trade unionists and the public generally will see that, having almest 200 men to maintain, the s-rugele means life or death to us, for if unsuccessful we shall be more degraiel than at present (which is bad eneue.h) ; hut if successful there will he a happier anl a brighter future before us. Should any lodge or branch of trade union- ists, or any trades c onacil, desire one or more of our membsrs to wait upon them for the purpose of explain- ing in greater detail our position we shall be pleased to comply with such request. Hoping and believing that our appeal will not he in vain. We are yours faithfully, JOSEPH DA.GNALL,Pre3ideflt; JAMES BATTERsßY,Secretary, 22, Cartwright-street. Mr. T. RIGBY said that as one of the deputation from the Filesmith's Association, be might say that that was the 126 week they had been on strike. They were not only fighting for a small advance in wages, but for their rights combined. (Hear, hear.) They had done all in their power to meet the roasters on fair terms.and had offered suggestions to them, but they (the masters) did not suggest anything to them. They had gone to them by inviration and otherwise to see if terms c ,uld not be arranged. It was a fact that many men worked for low wages whilst others did fairly well. It was their idea, aad it had been their idea all along, to evalies the rate of wages snore than it had been bafore. (Hear, hear.) They had suggested to the masters that they took a entrain percentage from men who did the very best class of work, and gave it to those who did the very worst— (Hear, hear) which they considered was a fair sugges- tion. The masters, however, objected to doing so, and Said trade was not good enough to allow them to give an advance of wages, but they (the men) thoueht trade wasisufficiently good.—(Hear. hear).—All other trades had advanced whilst they kept in the same position. They were fairly well supported throughout the country, and what they wanted was the support of bigger societies. (Hear, bear.) Various societies were levying their men, and a small amouat to them would mean a lot to Filesmiths. If a large society put on a small levy, it would keep them for a considerable length of time. They had received contributions from different Trades Councils, but he was sorry to Ray that Council seemed to be larking in that respect. Be did not know exactly whet the state of affairs was financially, but he did not see why that Council should not assist them as well as ethers.

Mr. GREALY thought the best thing to do would be to pass a re,iolution reqirrsting the secrPtary to write to the secretaries of the different societies represented in the Council, desiring thf.m to lay the facts of the CARO before the members, and ask them to support the filesnriths whilst they were en strike. (Hear. hear.) At this point, Mr. J. BROWN said he had been told by a pers-m well-known to the committee, that what the fil(9miths were telling the public was false. He wanted to have the matter put right. • Mr. GREALY 7.thoucibt that the statement should not be heard unless the name of the informant was given.

Mr. BRAWN did not think it would be wine to tell the name. Continuing he said that he was informed that the:filesmitbs in Warrington were not receiving on an average £1 a weekelome being in receipt of over £2. There were a number who made small files and they received £1 a week and even less. What he wanted to know was if they wanted them to understand that the average rate of nay was £1 or more than that. Mr. RIGBY said there were one or two present who could answer that question better than he. He asked them to allow another member of the deputation to reply. Mr. ROBERTS wes asked to answer the question. He said he had been a file cetter for forty years and there- fore thought he had had sufficient expeiience to under- stand what file-cutting w.s. As Mr. 'Rigby had said, there were some who did fairly well. On the contrary others only earned £1 a week, and they were trying to equalize the wages. (Hear, hear.) If a man got £2 a week, the work was exceptionally good, and the week had been an exceptional one. Going to extremes in one direction they might go to extremes in another. They found men earning only 14e a week. He had been challenged about the same question before by a man who said he knew someone getting £2 a week. He asked him what time he worked till and he said till 8 o'cleack on ordinary nights and 11 oteleck on Friday. If they reckoned up they would find that was 80 hours a week in all. He did not call that a week he thought it was a week and a half. (Hear, hear.) Their object was to raise the wages of those who were getting No little and they had sympathisers with that object. There were men who had gone in not sat'sfied with trying to rob them of their rights, but were trying to hinder them whilst they were fiehting. He went in one of the works with a deputa- tion last week and men had made fun of them Efil should like to know who it was that told Mr. Brown ; h. was certain it would be one of the men who was try- ing to defeat them. They were willing to'submit their case to arbitration. Men who were turning out the best of work gee 30s. and 355. a week ; a man had gone back to work that day who had proved himself a traitor from the beginning, and had handicapped them ever since ttey went en strike. He would ray he could ant 35g. a wedc, hut he had to work till 10 or 11 o'clock t night. He said ar!ain the was not working for a week but a ',reek and a half.

STOCKTON HEATH.

REVISING THE LISTS.—On Saturday afternoon, Mr. Clement E. Lloyd attended at the Club Room of the Lnridon Bridge Inn. Stockton Heath to revise the lists in the Appleton polling district, which com- prises the townships of Appleton-with-Bull, Stockton Beath, and Stretton. Mr. H. J. Westbrook and Mr. Dean represented the Conservatives, and Mr. Smith the Liberals. Mr. Edwin Bradshaw, assistant overseer, was present as representing Stockton Heath, and Mr. Jobh Ellison as representing Appleton and Stretton. Except the officials only one or two persons were present, and the proceedings were devoid of public intereAt.

P.C. Schofield proved the charge. PRIXEITITE METHODIST BAND OF HOPE, EARLESTOWN.

The winter session of this society was opened by a public meeting on Wednesday evening in the Primitive Methodist schoolroom. The Rev. E. Bocock presided, and there was a very fair attend- ance. The Chairman offered a few remarks by way of opening, and an address on Temperance was given by Mr. W. H. Dale. A programme of songs and recitati ons was very pleasingly rendered by Misses Baxter, 1 ullins, Woods, Rawlins,Pendry and Harrison.

1.0. G T. HOPE OF EARLESTOWN LODGE. A mestiPg of ti is: odge;was held in the Co-operative Hall, Wargrave-road, on Monday evening, Brother Francis, C.T. presiding. After the usual opening services, one membcr was re-obligated, Sic Francis and Sic Grant being appointed as the regalia com- mittee, The Secretary reported that Sister Duncan, P.D.S.G.T. had accepted the invitation to speak at the Juvenile a nniversary. The programme was a, night with the convention officers. Bro Francis C.T. then called on Bro Roberts,P.C.C.T., Bro Sheath of Warrington Bro and Sic Turner of St. Helens,and Bro Battersby of Leigh, each of whom briefly des- cribed the state of the Order and of the temperance cause:in their district and the efforts that were be- ing made,Bro Turner also describing the work in the Army. Refreshments were handed round by Sister Warburton and Sister Ord.

NEWTON T. PETER'S CHURCH, BIBLE CLASS. The first ot a series of socials was held in St. Peter's Schools on Saturday evening in con- nection with the newly formed Young Men's Bible Class which is to be conducted by the Rev. James Rider, the Vicar of St. Peters. There were about 80 present including the Vicar and church officers. The proceedings were initiated at 6 o'clock by a substantial knife and fork tea, which it is needless to say, was fully enjoyed. After tea games of vari- ous kinds and dancing, were indulged in, the in- tervalsnbeing filled up with singing, in which Messrs. DeVereUX, Grace, Perry and Miss Wilkinson took part. The Rev. James Rider in a very brief address congratulated the Committee upon the suc- cess of their first gathering and referred in promis- ing terms to the proposed Blble class. Mr. Single- ton also spoke, and a thoroughly enjoyable evening terminated about 10-30. Mr. P. Fairclough officiated at the piano. BARLESTOWN WESLEYAN CRICKET CLUB.

A general meeting of the above club was held in the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Monday evening. The reason the meeting has been held so early, is in order that a secretary could be appointed, to arrange fixtures for next season, a great deal of in- convenience having been -experienced during the past season, by the previous secretary booking wrong dates for fixtures, &c. Mr. E. Wolfe was elected to the chair, anti the temporary secretary (Mr. Joeph Anderton) proceeded to give a short re- view of the past season's work. The club have had a very succastsful season only two matches having been lost, and in one of these they were handi- capped by several of their best players being absent. The election of officers was next proceeded with, and resulted as follows :—Committee, Messrs 6, Robitiv,n, P Talbot, II Clare, J Roberts, James Marsh, C Broster, Joseph Marsh, T Farthing, and T Clare; Treasurer, Mr E Goblin ; Secretary, Mr J Yorke; Assistant Secretary, Mr C F Porter; Captain, Ist team, Mr E Goslin ; Vice- Captain, Mr J Anderton ; Captain, 2nd team, Mr F Farthing; Vice-Captain, Mr F Clare. It was decided that the membership fees for next season shall be:— Adults, playing members, 3s. Non-playing members 35., boys (under elghteen) 2s. The question as to the playing ground was raised but it was eventually decided to leave the matter in the hands of the new committee.

From now to the finish the play became very rough, one payer of Newton's being ordered off the field, and several on both sides had to be cautioned for exhibiting considerable feeling. Having only ten men to contend with, the visitors EOM assumed the aggressive, and Deno eventually made the scores level. The play now became fast and exciting, both teams striving hard to gain the lead, but for a time the efforts on each side were nullified by fine defensive tac- tics. Harrison scored,the home team winning a pal ten minutes off time. Final: Newton, 3; Flint, 2.
